- if you refer to a function, or datastructure, or file by name, I will have no idea what you're talking about.
- gabrilend is the kind of computer scientist that reads a lot of theory about computers, but doesn't handle symbols very well. She understands how a threadpool works, but she has no idea what thread_pool_operation_manager_handle() does. Such terms are useless to her.
- the only place we need to update an issue file when the issue is partially complete is the "current behavior" section. We don't need to add append-logs to the end, it's better to update the main body to align with the current design, and the current behavior with the current status.

```

### feedback_comprehensive_first_pass.md

```markdown
---
name: Don't tier work into cheap / medium / expensive
description: For new features, do every fold of the work in one comprehensive slice rather than splitting into easy-now / hard-later tiers
type: feedback
originSessionId: 1c5a19da-96cd-4e77-88ea-625e76d201fa
---
When scoping a new feature, resist the instinct to propose a
"slice 1 covers cheap fields; slice 2 covers expensive fields"
split unless the user explicitly asks for it.

**Why:** The user's framing: "they should all be configurable
with the same interface, so... don't split up the work in that
way. Just do it comprehensively the first time so we don't
forget anything on the return trip." Tiered work means coming
back, and round-trips are where details get forgotten —
especially the cross-cutting plumbing that connects the easy
and hard paths.

**How to apply:** When writing an issue file, if a feature
spans cheap-and-hard variations of the same surface (e.g. all
fields of a schema, all kinds of a polymorphic type), describe
slice 1 as covering the whole surface. The implementation may
still ship incrementally on a per-commit basis, but the issue
should not encode a "we'll do the rest later" plan unless the
user has explicitly asked for that staging.

```

### feedback_cross_language_pitfalls.md

```markdown
---
name: Cross-language design pitfalls
description: Three approaches the user rejected when designing SoraMech's cross-language wire format
type: feedback
originSessionId: e9ed70c7-2b63-425c-987c-6431040bedfb
---
When designing anything that touches the cross-language data path, avoid these three shapes — the user pushed back on each and the rejections are load-bearing for the architecture.

**1. Don't propose runtime "handles" that route operations back to the originating spec.**

**Why:** Real library functions in the wild (qsort, libcurl, anything from libc) expect actual native values — a real `int (*)(int,int)` function pointer, a real `char *` — not a SoraMech-issued id string. They can't be modified to participate in our runtime's routing scheme. The wire must carry data the destination spec can fully reconstruct into a native value, not opaque tokens.

**How to apply:** When a value seems hard to serialize (function pointers, coroutines, opaque pointers), decompose to data primitives (address + signature, bytecode + upvalues, ptr + length). If the destination needs a callable, the destination spec synthesizes one at reconstruction time (libffi trampoline for C, `ffi.cast` for LuaJIT). The "phone home" callback that the trampoline performs internally is fine — it's an artifact of the destination spec's reconstruction, not exposed in the wire format. What's forbidden is a wire primitive that says "operations on this value cross back to the source."

**2. Don't propose pair-wise language conversion paths (lua→c, c→lua, etc.).**

**Why:** N languages means N² conversion paths, which means adding a new language breaks the existing ones. The wire must use a language-agnostic intermediate (JSON) and every spec must implement only the two pair-symmetric callbacks (`to_json`, `from_json`). New language = implement those two functions, nothing else touches.

**How to apply:** Never write code that names two specific languages in a conversion context. Specs only ever see JSON on the wire. If a JSON primitive needs an extension (like `$function_pointer`), it lives in the universal JSON contract, not as a lua-spec-specific or c-spec-specific concept.

**3. Don't propose per-function annotations for type ambiguities (lengths, discriminators, callback signatures).**

**Why:** The user pointed out that "discriminator" is a vocab word the system has no general way to ask about. We can't prompt "which param is the length?" because that pairing convention lives in the function's English documentation, not its types. Asking the user to write annotations means asking them to know specialized vocabulary they didn't sign up for.

**How to apply:** When a function signature has ambiguous types (`char *` with no length, `union` with no tag, `void *` with no signature), expose each parameter as a separate input slot on the box. The user wires the slots according to the function's documentation — `p` from one source, `n` from another. The system never infers pairings; the user expresses them by wiring. If the destination needs a length and no length wire is provided, the wire compile fails with a plain-English fix-it message ("destination needs length, source provides only a pointer — wire a length input here").

**Common thread:** all three rejections are the same principle in different clothes — **SoraMech bends to the language, not the other way around.** Users write idiomatic code in their language. Libraries are used as-is. Specs are self-contained and pair-symmetric. The system stays out of the user's vocabulary.

```

### project_cross_language_wire.md

```markdown
---
name: Cross-language wire format
description: Dual-ring slot per port (native ring + JSON ring + ordering ring). JSON only at language borders. Per-edge classification at graph load; per-cell precision at runtime.
type: project
originSessionId: 5670858b-274f-49c4-89b8-a4b83ce33d7c
---
Design corrected 2026-05-21 — replaces the older "JSON always" framing.

**Each input slot is a dual-ring buffer.** Per port: one `ring_native` (consumer-language native bytes), one `ring_json` (JSON bytes from cross-language producers), and one `ring_order` of `(which_ring, idx)` tuples in arrival order. Allocate `ring_json` and `ring_order` lazily — same-language-only ports pay nothing for them.

**Push selects ring by per-edge bit.** Producer's spec writes native bytes to `ring_native` if `output_edge_native[j]` is true; otherwise calls `native_to_json` and writes to `ring_json`. Either way, pushes a `(which_ring, idx)` tuple to `ring_order`.

**Pop reads ordering ring first.** Dispatch pops `ring_order`, gets `(which_ring, idx)`, then pops the indicated cell. The consumer's spec receives `(input_data[i], input_native[i])` per input port and dispatches per flag — decode native or decode JSON.

**Why dual-ring instead of per-port classification.** Per-port (slot is all-JSON if any fan-in is cross-lang) is the pessimistic approximation — same-language values eat JSON cost on mixed-fan-in ports. Dual-ring is precise per cell: a port fed by one Lua box and one C box keeps the Lua cells native and JSON-encodes only the C-sourced cells.

**Why JSON for the cross-language ring (not pair-wise paths, not type-annotated raw bytes).** JSON's structure is self-describing — every language has a natural mapping for object/array/number/string/bool/null. Adding language K means K's spec implements its own `native_to_json` and `json_to_native`. N spec-pairs, not N² language-pair paths. The native ring's format is each spec's private choice — Lua may pick msgpack or `cjson.encode` or a custom binary form; C uses `memcpy` of the wrapper struct; Bash uses strings.

**Sentinel primitives for non-JSON-mappable values.** `$ref` (pointer + length into the large-value heap), `$function_pointer`, `$lang_opaque` ("these bytes are language-X-internal"). Carried inside the JSON ring as object-shaped sentinels. Cross-language receivers that can't reconstruct fail at wire compile.

**`$function_pointer` reconstruction — wrapper binary + persistent subprocess (issue 318, amended 2026-05-22).** Earlier design had the consumer's spec FFI-cast the raw process-local address. Rejected: FFI is language-pair-specific, biases SoraMech toward the Lua↔C corner, breaks the N-not-N² rule. Replacement: at map-compile time the compiler asks the *producing* spec to compile the referenced function + its enumerable dependencies into a standalone wrapper binary on disk that speaks a small line protocol on stdio. The consumer spec spawns one persistent subprocess per `$function_pointer` it receives and talks to it over the same socket-like channel used for any out-of-process spec. Every language pays the same price; new languages only implement "compile a function to a wrapper binary" — no pair-wise FFI knowledge required.

**Spec `invoke` signature change.** Collapses `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` into one `invoke` with per-input/per-output flags: `int invoke(handle, ..., const int *input_native, int n_inputs, int output_native, void *out_buf, ...)`. The spec dispatches per input internally. This is the contract change that unblocks 306/307's typed marshalling.

**Two compile-time layers stack:**
- **Per-edge classification (issue 312, this design)** — graph loader computes `input_edge_native[]` and `output_edge_native[]` on each box; dispatch routes pushes accordingly. This issue is the wire-format design.
- **Same-language source merge (issue 313, research / blocked)** — concatenate every same-language box's source into one translation unit per language. Boxes still run as separate thread-pool tasks; what 313 thins is the spec invocation overhead inside the merged region (direct call into the worker's `lua_State` instead of dlsym + marshal + bridge). The dual-ring slot machinery does NOT collapse — tasks still need slots to wait for sibling inputs. 313 makes the ring cells cheaper to read/write; it doesn't remove them.

**Three rejected design shapes** (kept here so they don't get reinvented):
1. **Handles back-routing to the owning spec** — library functions in the wild can't be modified to use SoraMech-shaped handles. Rejected.
2. **Pair-wise language paths** — every new language would have to grow N-1 translation paths against existing languages. Rejected. The dual-ring's "two rings per slot" is the dimension-cap that prevents this.
3. **Per-function discriminator / length annotations on the wire** — the system has no universal vocabulary to ask "is param 2 the length?" Rejected; the user expresses these by wiring two separate input slots.

**How to apply:** When discussing wire format, slot store shape, dispatch routing, or spec callbacks: think dual-ring (native, JSON, ordering) per slot, per-edge classification at push time, per-cell tag at pop time, JSON-only-at-borders, language-private native bytes everywhere else.

```

### project_custom_translation_specs.md

```markdown
---
name: Custom translation specs per port
description: Users can override the default native/JSON wire path on any input port for any reason — a knob in the wire format vocabulary (issue 246)
type: project
originSessionId: 1228a221-1c09-4c26-94f3-172d7777b2b9
---
**Per-port custom translation specs** let the user intervene at a specific input port and replace the default dual-ring decode path with user-written code. The override applies to that one port; the rest of the graph uses the default native-or-JSON path from issue 312.

**Motivating cases (any reason qualifies, not just cross-language):**
- Cross-language ambiguity the default specs can't infer: a C++ class with methods landing at a C box (drop methods? expose as fn pointers? restructure?), a `void *` carrying a libcurl handle, a union whose discriminator is on a separate wire.
- Same-language reshape: massaging a value into a different shape on its way in (rename fields, drop a layer, project a subset) without adding a transform box on the canvas.
- Validation / coercion: clamp a number, enforce a schema, fail loudly on unexpected shapes.

**How it composes with the dual-ring design:**
- The custom spec runs at *pop* time on the consumer side, after the ordering ring has been consulted and the cell has been read from the appropriate ring. The user's code receives the decoded value (native or JSON-decoded) and produces whatever the consumer's `invoke` will see.
- A port with a custom translation gets its own wire color in the editor (issue 245) — visible signal that this port doesn't use the default path.

**Why:** The default native/JSON contract covers the cases JSON's structure handles cleanly. Everything outside that needs a user escape hatch — *and* the same hatch is useful for purely-local "I want this input shaped differently" cases. One mechanism, many uses.

**How to apply:** When the design needs an override knob on the cross-language path, propose "custom translation spec on the destination port" rather than inventing per-function annotations, type discriminator metadata, or producer-side conversion hints (all three were rejected — see feedback_cross_language_pitfalls). The port is the unit of override; the spec is user code; the rest stays default.

```

### project_editor_visual_language.md

```markdown
---
name: Editor visual language for wires
description: Wire color in the canvas editor encodes language pairing across the edge, not just routing kind — a UI invariant future work should respect (issue 245)
type: project
originSessionId: 1228a221-1c09-4c26-94f3-172d7777b2b9
---
**Wires are colored by language pairing across the edge**, with a separate color for wires whose destination port carries a user-written custom translation (issue 246). Pre-245 wires were colored purely by routing kind (default slate-blue, comparator branches orange/blue/green).

**The pairing-based palette:**
- **Same-language hop** — the wire carries native bytes; default neutral color.
- **Cross-language hop (forest green, ~`#228b22`)** — different producer/consumer languages; runtime translates via JSON at the wire. The color is a standing reminder that encode + decode is happening on that hop.
- **Custom-translation hop** — a third distinct color signaling the destination port has a user-written translation spec overriding the default path.
- Branch-routing colors (orange/blue/green from comparator boxes) compose on top — routing kind and language pairing are orthogonal axes the editor must show simultaneously.

**Why:** The runtime treats same-lang and cross-lang hops very differently (dual-ring slot design, issue 312), but pre-245 the editor gave the box author no visual signal that the JSON translation step existed. It was invisible until someone read the dispatch code. Translation cost should be legible from the canvas.

**How to apply:** Any future editor work that touches wire rendering must preserve "color encodes language pairing." Don't propose recoloring schemes that overload the cross-language signal onto another concept. New routing concepts get new axes (line style, glyphs, animation) rather than stealing color. When discussing wire visuals, name both axes — routing kind AND language pairing — even if only one is changing.

```

### project_encapsulated_map.md

```markdown
---
name: Encapsulated map as box
description: A sub-map opens as a single box on a parent canvas. Data boxes marked externally-supplied become input ports; write boxes marked externally-consumed become output ports. The documented exception to single-output-per-box (issue 248, amended 2026-05-23)
type: project
originSessionId: 1228a221-1c09-4c26-94f3-172d7777b2b9
---
**A map directory has two ways to be opened:** as the top-level graph (existing map-select UI), or as an **encapsulated box** on the currently-edited parent map (issue 248). Opening through the file browser while a parent map is open instantiates the sub-map as a single box on the parent's canvas.

**The encapsulation contract — symmetric input and output toggles:**
- **Inputs:** any `data` box inside the sub-map can be toggled "externally-supplied" in its inspector. Each such box becomes a typed input port on the encapsulated box.
- **Outputs:** any `write` box inside the sub-map can be toggled "externally-consumed" in its inspector. Each such box surfaces as a **distinct output port** on the encapsulated box. A write box with the toggle on can still write to its `path` if one is set — the file destination and the parent-wire destination are independent.

**Encapsulated maps are the documented exception to issue 218's "single output per box" rule.** They have *N* output ports — one per externally-consumed write box, where *N* may be zero (pure side-effect map), one, or many. Every other box kind still has exactly one output.

**Rejected earlier design (2026-05-21):** packing all write-box outputs into a single byte-blob with a leading null-terminated size index, single wire out. Rejected 2026-05-23 because it would have repurposed file-writing machinery to emit strings into wires — substituting one mechanism for another. The symmetric toggle design preserves the `write` box's "terminate a value" semantics and treats the destination kind as a mode toggle.

**Why:** Encapsulation is a function-like abstraction over a sub-graph. The parent doesn't see the sub-map's internals — it sees declared input ports, declared output ports, and known semantics. Sub-maps can be edited independently, reused across parents, and packaged once for distribution. This is the closest SoraMech gets to "user-defined functions" while staying graph-native.

**How to apply:** When discussing map composition, reuse, or "how do users build libraries of behavior" — encapsulation is the answer. Don't propose alternative function-abstraction mechanisms (named subgraphs as text references, macro-style inlining, blob-packed outputs) without explicitly weighing them against the symmetric-toggle shape. Both toggles (`externally-supplied` on data boxes, `externally-consumed` on write boxes) are the user-facing port-declaration mechanism — don't invent a separate "ports manifest" file.

```

### project_soramech.md

```markdown
---
name: SoraMech project context
description: Core architecture decisions, file layout, and execution model for SoraMech, across phase 2 (current) and phase 3 (designed)
type: project
originSessionId: 579d58b3-7452-44f3-983a-80ff5821082a
---
SoraMech is a visual FSM/dataflow editor and execution engine at /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/.

**Why:** Primary use case is LLM orchestration on the SoraMind Alpine cluster. Strings flow between boxes like variables. History is explicit, loaded from disk only when a box needs it.

**Three independent programs sharing a map directory:**
- `src/006-server-main.lua` — thin HTTP file CRUD proxy. No runner management.
- Runner — phase 2: `src/007-runner-main.lua` (synchronous Lua). Phase 3: `soramech-pool` (C binary, designed in issues 301–311, not yet built).
- `assets/index.html` — vanilla JS canvas editor. Editor only — no run button. Compile button (issue 222) is a phase 2 placeholder; it triggers the phase 3 build pipeline (issue 219).

**Key architecture decisions (current truth):**
- Each box is its own JSON file in `boxes/<id>.json`. Connections written to BOTH endpoint files; loader validates both ends agree — hard error if not.
- **Single output wire per box** (issue 218 complete). No `outputs` array. The function returns one value; routing is by `from_branch` on the connection.
- **Comparator-based branching** (issue 210) — replaces the earlier `branch` box kind with named ports. A box may carry a `comparand`; the dispatch layer fires `lt`/`eq`/`gt` connections. No spec is invoked for comparators.
- **Iterator boxes** (issue 221) — pure routing primitives, no `ref`/`fn`. Dispatch layer cycles through `iterator_outputs` based on a per-task counter.
- **Phase 2 drivers** are shell scripts under `drivers/` (one process per call, stdout as channel). **Phase 3 language specs** are `.so` libraries under `langs/<name>/spec.so` loaded via `dlopen` (issue 303). Lua and C are in-process; Bash is out-of-process via Unix domain socket.
- Data files: per-map JSON in `data/`. Section-level `constant` flag. Accessed via `libs/soramech-data.lua`.
- State/ephemeral: `tmp/` is a symlink to `/tmp/<map-name>/`. Phase 2 writes `last-run.json` (single doc). Phase 3 writes `last-run.jsonl` (JSON Lines, issue 311) — decoupled from the editor, consumed by LLMs / CLI viewers / test harness.
- **Phase 3 runtime model**: C pool runner owns a 3d-rts task pool; per-task slots in process heap (issue 302) with refcounting and a separate large-value heap; dispatch layer (304) is the worker action; graph loader is C (305), no embedded Lua at the runner level. Phase 2's synchronous executor retires when phase 3 lands.

**How to apply:** When working on any component, respect the three-way separation. Server never knows about runner. Runner never knows about server. Map directory format is the only shared contract. The phase 2 driver model and the phase 3 spec model coexist conceptually (phase 2 is current, phase 3 is fully designed) but the runtime mechanism differs entirely; box JSON and the editor are unchanged across the cutover.

```

### project_threading.md

```markdown
---
name: threading_model
description: SoraMech's threading model — phase 2 synchronous, phase 3 SoraMech-owned C thread pool with per-port slots and language specs
type: project
originSessionId: 579d58b3-7452-44f3-983a-80ff5821082a
---
**Phase 2 (current)**: synchronous executor in `src/004-executor.lua`. Single-threaded, sequential box execution. One driver shell-out per box call. The phase 2 path goes away when phase 3 lands — no fallback, no coroutine intermediate.

**Phase 3 (designed, not yet built)**: C pool runner with a SoraMech-owned thread pool. The 3d-rts pool at `/home/ritz/programming/ai-stuff/games/3d-rts/libs/900-task-pool.h` is the **design reference**, not vendored — SoraMech owns its pool implementation so the spawn primitive, worker context, init barrier, and iterator-pinning extensions are all under our control. The architecture is fully specified across issues 301–313.

Key phase 3 concepts:
- **Per-input-port slots** (not per-task), allocated at graph load and durable for the run. Each slot is a ring buffer (`n_cells` cells of `cell_capacity` bytes). 1-cell slots use peek-not-drain (literals, single-push wires); N-cell slots use pop-and-drain (queued / multi-push wires). No per-slot refcounting — slot lifetime is the whole run.
- **Tasks are ephemeral**: spawned dynamically by the dispatch layer when a box's input set is ready. Tasks carry just `box_id` and a counter snapshot. Box state (counter, input slots, language handle) is durable.
- **Language specs** under `langs/<name>/spec.so`, loaded via `dlopen` at startup. Uniform `lang_spec_t`. No language is privileged.
- **Same-language fast path** (issue 312): wire format defaults to JSON, but two adjacent same-language boxes use the language's native serialization (Lua `lua_dump`, C struct memcpy). Compile-time wire classification picks the path.
- **In-process specs** (Lua, C) via `lua_pcall` / `dlsym`. **Out-of-process specs** (Bash) via Unix domain sockets to a persistent per-worker subprocess.
- **Dispatch layer** (304) is the worker-side action: pop inputs → branch on box mode → invoke spec → push to downstream input slots → end. No blocking, no wait lists; tasks spawn on input arrival and run to completion.
- **Iterator and comparator routing** in the dispatch layer. Comparator runs the function and routes by output value. Iterator skips the function and routes by per-box counter (atomic spawn-time increment). Iterator tasks parallelize across workers; ordering preserved via per-push counter tags on consumer slots.
- **No-null rule** (issue 230): every input port must be wired, given a literal, or marked optional. Compile fails on missing inputs.
- **Init barrier**: pool startup waits until every worker has run every spec's `init(worker_idx)` before submitting tasks. Avoids tasks landing on uninitialized handles.
- **Hard-crash error policy**: nonzero return from any spec aborts the program.
- **Blocking semantics**: long-running operations tie up a worker. Pool size is the budget. Cooperative split (kickoff + check-done iterator) avoids holding a worker. See `docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md`.

**Effil is explicitly NOT used.** Coroutines are not used. The C pool is the chosen mechanism. 3d-rts is referenced as design inspiration, not vendored.

**How to apply:** When discussing threading, parallelism, or cross-language IPC: phase 2 is synchronous shell-out drivers; phase 3 is the SoraMech-owned C pool runner with `lang_spec_t`, per-port slot stores, and dispatch-layer routing. Never suggest effil. 3d-rts is a reference, not a dependency. Issues 301–313 carry the design.

```

### project_yarq_and_third_ring.md

```markdown
---
name: YARQ and the third input ring (issue 320)
description: Per-port third ring carries box-JSON reconfigure messages; YARQ (yield-and-requeue) is the project name for the cooperative-with-scheduler barrier that protects in-place box mutation
type: project
originSessionId: 1c5a19da-96cd-4e77-88ea-625e76d201fa
---
Issue 320 introduces two pieces of project vocabulary that
recur in other code-paths:

**YARQ** (yield-and-requeue) — the synchronization primitive
that protects in-place box mutation. Acquire is a CAS from 0
to 1 on a per-box atomic word. On acquire failure, the
contended worker does NOT busy-wait — it re-submits its own
task to the **tail** of the pool queue and returns, letting
the scheduler drain other work first. Same barrier is acquired
by every path that mutates a box's record (reconfigure,
runtime_connect from 319d). The point: synchronization
*cooperative with the scheduler*, not adversarial to it.
The user named it; the joke is that they want to name a dog
YARQ too.

**The third input ring** — every input slot grows a third ring
alongside the native + JSON pair from issue 312. Carries
box-JSON-shaped reconfigure messages. Per-PORT placement (not
per-box) — any input port can receive reconfigure cells, and
the per-cell ring-selector tag from issue 312/318a grows a
third value (`RING_RECONFIGURE`). When the consumer's
input-gather encounters a reconfigure cell, it retains
already-popped values, acquires YARQ, applies the reconfigure,
releases YARQ, and resumes gathering under the new config.

**Why:** Together with issue 319 (create_box / connect),
issue 320 gives the runtime its full "graph as a living
artifact" surface: 319 grows the graph (additive), 320
mutates what's already there (in-place). Many future features
will read or write these primitives — runtime DSLs, planner
boxes, the editor's live-mutation feedback path.

**How to apply:** When other issues touch box mutation,
slot rings, or per-cell ordering, treat YARQ and the third
ring as established vocabulary — don't reinvent the
primitive or the name. When the user mentions "reconfigure,"
"the third ring," or YARQ, they mean these specific pieces
of the design.

```

### reference_strategems.md

```markdown
---
name: Strategems directory

**Location:** `/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/strategems/`

**What it is:** Files in this directory are **distilled design rules** the user has named after a pattern that recurs in many places. They're shorter than docs/, longer than a one-line CLAUDE.md instruction, and load-bearing for design judgment. Each file is one strategem — a rule with a memorable phrase as the filename. The phrase-as-filename is the strategem's name; use it as shared vocabulary.

**Current strategems (as of 2026-05-23):**
- `commit-language-is-runtime-language.md`
- `contracts-must-fit-every-implementer.md`
- `redesigns-change-the-surface-too.md`

**How to apply (the user's preference is encouragement, not force):**
- **Re-list the directory once per context** (`ls strategems/`) so the current set is in my working memory. Don't auto-read each one — let the filenames sit there as triggers.
- When a filename *feels* relevant to the task at hand, read it before proceeding. Relevance signals: I'm about to propose a contract, a redesign, a language-spec change, or anything where a recurring principle might apply. If nothing feels relevant, don't force a reach.
- Re-list at session start (or first time the project surfaces) — new strategems land here without ceremony and the list above may be stale across sessions.
- When a strategem does apply, name it explicitly to the user using its filename phrase. That's the shared vocabulary the naming is for.
- If during a session the user expresses a rule in the form "X must always Y because Z" and the rule generalizes beyond the current task, suggest writing it as a strategem rather than only as a memory.

**Recent commit pattern:** strategem files are committed individually (e.g. `c57c719 strategems: contracts must fit every implementer, not just the simplest`), so `git log -- strategems/` is the fastest way to see what's been added lately and the reasoning that prompted it.

## [13]

```json
{
  "cwd":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech",
  "type":"user",
  "timestamp":"2026-05-25T15:39:48.510Z",
  "gitBranch":"master",

    "role":"user",
    "content":[{
        "tool_use_id":"toolu_01XSAMGHZmBCyRrHWmjuCE8G",
        "type":"tool_result",
        "content":"1\t# 320 — Box reconfigure via the utility-box kind\n2\t\n3\t## Status\n4\topen · design substantially settled; a few naming / mechanical\n5\tdetails still need a fresh-morning pass (see \"Open items for\n6\tlater\" at the bottom)\n7\t\n8\t## Current behavior\n9\t\n10\tA box's identity is frozen at graph-load. The loader parses\n11\t`boxes/<id>.json` once, fills in every field — kind, language,\n12\tfunction pointer, input port list, routing shape, outgoing\n13\tconnections — and the dispatch layer reads that record on every\n14\tfire. Nothing in the running program changes a box's record.\n15\t\n16\tIssue 319f already added a language-agnostic way to **create**\n17\tnew boxes at runtime: the BOX_CREATE_BOX kind. A box of that\n18\tkind takes a JSON spec on a `spec` input port; when it fires, it\n19\tparses the spec, allocates the new box at a fresh graph slot,\n20\tand emits the new id as a string on its output wire. Downstream\n21\tconsumers can take the id and wire the new box up further (via\n22\tBOX_CONNECT).\n23\t\n24\tThis issue extends that pattern to **reconfigure** and\n25\t**delete** — also via a box kind, not via library functions in\n26\tthe language bridges. The new mechanism is the utility-box: a\n27\tsingle box kind that subsumes create / reconfigure / delete by\n28\tinspecting the spec it receives.\n29\t\n30\t## Architectural ground rules (from the design discussion)\n31\t\n32\t1. **Graph mutation is a box-kind operation, not a language-\n33\t   bridge function.** Users express mutations by placing\n34\t   utility-boxes in their maps and wiring them, not by writing\n35\t   code that imports the runtime as a library. The runtime is\n36\t   the substrate; users express through it.\n37\t2. **Manual JSON construction is supported but not assisted.**\n38\t   A user who wants to build a long-running Lua box that\n39\t   accumulates JSON for its own future reconfigure can do so —\n40\t   the manual path works as long as the bytes end up on a wire\n41\t   that feeds a utility-box. We don't provide convenience\n42\t   helpers for this path; the box-and-wire path is the intended\n43\t   one.\n44\t3. **The wire endpoint is the target.** When a utility-box's\n45\t   output wire points at box X's input port, that wire IS the\n46\t   declaration of \"this utility-box operates on box X.\" No\n47\t   separate target_id input.\n48\t4. **One unified box kind.** create / reconfigure / delete are\n49\t   all the same kind, dispatched by the spec's content. Folds\n50\t   in 319f's BOX_CREATE_BOX.\n51\t5. **Reconfigures ride the regular input slots, tagged with\n52\t   RING_RECONFIGURE.** No separate control queue. The gather\n53\t   lock makes peek-then-pop honest within the lock, so the\n54\t   peek-vs-pop race that would have plagued an unsynchronised\n55\t   design doesn't appear.\n56\t\n57\t## The five structural pieces\n58\t\n59\t### 1. The utility-box kind\n60\t\n61\tOne box kind absorbs 319f's BOX_CREATE_BOX. The behaviour\n62\tdepends on the spec's content:\n63\t\n64\t- **Spec describes a box with no existing target** (or target\n65\t  doesn't exist in the graph): CREATE. Allocate at a fresh\n66\t  graph slot. Emit the new id as a string on the output wire.\n67\t- **Spec describes a box with an existing target id** (the\n68\t  wire's endpoint matches an existing box): RECONFIGURE.\n69\t  Build a replacement box struct. Push the struct pointer to\n70\t  the target box's input slot (whichever port the wire ends\n71\t  at), tagged RING_RECONFIGURE.\n72\t- **Spec is empty / minimal** (no fields, no function): DELETE\n73\t  signal. Push a delete-marker to the target box's input slot,\n74\t  tagged RING_DELETE (or maybe just RING_RECONFIGURE with a\n75\t  null struct — see open items).\n76\t\n77\t### 2. The gather flag\n78\t\n79\tEach box record carries one atomic int — the gather flag — with\n80\ttwo states:\n81\t\n82\t- **0 (free):** any worker may attempt to enter.\n83\t- **1 (held):** exactly one worker is currently in the gather\n84\t  section.\n85\t\n86\tWorkers entering a box CAS from 0 to 1. Win → enter gather.\n87\tLose → yield-and-requeue (push task back to pool tail and\n88\treturn). No spinning, no blocking. Per-box, so workers on\n89\tdifferent boxes don't contend.\n90\t\n91\t### 3. The graph slot as liveness indicator\n92\t\n93\tThe graph stores boxes as an array of pointers, one per box id.\n94\t`graph_box(graph, id)` returns the current pointer. The mutator\n95\tatomically swaps that pointer from OLD to NEW. Workers that\n96\tLATER look up the same id get NEW; workers mid-fire on OLD\n97\tfinish on OLD.\n98\t\n99\tA box is **live** when the graph slot points at it. **Superseded**\n100\twhen the graph slot points at something else (or NULL, for\n101\tdelete). The two-state flag doesn't need a third \"redirect\"\n102\tstate because the graph slot itself is the source of truth for\n103\t\"is this still the current box.\"\n104\t\n105\t### 4. The reference count\n106\t\n107\tEach box record has an atomic refcount. Workers increment at\n108\tthe top of their fire (just after resolving the pointer through\n109\tthe graph slot) and decrement at the bottom. The mutator\n110\tincrements when it becomes the mutator, decrements after the\n111\tswap.\n112\t\n113\tFreeable when BOTH:\n114\t- Graph slot doesn't point at this box (superseded), AND\n115\t- Refcount has dropped to zero.\n116\t\n117\tWhoever decrements to zero AND observes the graph-slot\n118\tmismatch frees the box. Mutator's \"hold a +1\" discipline\n119\tensures the refcount only reaches zero AFTER the mutator\n120\tfinishes its work.\n121\t\n122\t### 5. The reconfigure tag on input slots\n123\t\n124\tEach input slot's ordering ring grows a RING_RECONFIGURE value\n125\talongside the existing RING_NATIVE and RING_JSON. When a worker\n126\tenters gather and peeks each input port's ordering ring, a\n127\tRING_RECONFIGURE tag at any port's head means \"this is a\n128\tcontrol message, not a value.\" The cell holds a pointer to a\n129\tpre-built box_t struct (built by the utility-box during its\n130\tfire, in the utility-box's worker thread — no parsing happens\n131\tinside the target's mutator path).\n132\t\n133\t## The worker arrival flow\n134\t\n135\t```\n136\t1. look up the graph slot for X → get current box pointer (B).\n137\t2. CAS B's gather flag from 0 to 1.\n138\t   - lose: yarq this task. exit.\n139\t   - win: continue.\n140\t3. increment B's refcount.\n141\t4. peek each of B's input ports' ordering rings.\n142\t5. branch on what's there:\n143\t     all heads are values:\n144\t        pop one ordering message + value from each port.\n145\t        release gather flag.\n146\t        call spec's invoke with the gathered values.\n147\t        push outputs to downstream slots per B's connections.\n148\t        decrement B's refcount; free if it qualifies.\n149\t\n150\t     any head is RING_RECONFIGURE:\n151\t        pop the struct pointer from that port.\n152\t        run mutator path (see below).\n153\t\n154\t     some ports empty:\n155\t        don't pop anything. release gather flag. exit.\n156\t        the pool will respawn this box when more values arrive.\n157\t```\n158\t\n159\t## The mutator flow\n160\t\n161\t```\n162\t1. with B's gather flag held and refcount +1:\n163\t2. pop the replacement struct pointer (call it NEW) from the\n164\t   RING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell.\n165\t3. resolve NEW's input_slot_ids by port-name matching:\n166\t     for each port in NEW:\n167\t       if same name exists in B (= OLD): copy that slot id.\n168\t       else: allocate a fresh slot.\n169\t4. record OLD's abandoned slots (in OLD but not in NEW). They\n170\t   get released when OLD is freed; remaining queued values\n171\t   emit a discard JSONL event per slot.\n172\t5. atomically swap the graph slot for the box id from OLD to NEW.\n173\t6. release B's gather flag.\n174\t7. yarq this task. (queued values on B's slots — still there,\n175\t   slot store didn't change — get fired under the new shape\n176\t   when the requeued task runs.)\n177\t8. decrement OLD's refcount; free OLD + abandoned slots if 0.\n178\t```\n179\t\n180\tThe yarq in step 7 is load-bearing — without it, queued values\n181\ton B's input slots sit unprocessed until the next producer\n182\tpush.\n183\t\n184\t## Slot diffing by port name\n185\t\n186\tSame algorithm as UI framework tree diffing: match by stable\n187\tidentifier (port name), only-in-old gets abandoned, only-in-new\n188\tgets freshly allocated, in-both keeps its slot id (and its\n189\tqueued values).\n190\t\n191\tRenames are NOT auto-detected — \"x\" → \"x_renamed\" looks like\n192\t\"delete x, create x_renamed.\" Any queued values on \"x\" get\n193\tdropped to JSONL. Value-preserving rename via explicit\n194\t`{\"rename\":[...]}` field is a possible future addition.\n195\t\n196\t## JSONL events\n197\t\n198\t- `box_reconfigure` — fires after the swap. Carries box id,\n199\t  post-apply spec (re-serialised from the new struct), task id,\n200\t  worker idx. Always emitted.\n201\t- `reconfigure_discard` — fires per abandoned port with leftover\n202\t  values. Carries box id, port name, count of dropped values,\n203\t  first value bytes (up to 256). Always emitted.\n204\t- `box_delete` — fires when the spec resolves to a delete.\n205\t  Carries box id, task id, worker idx. Always emitted.\n206\t\n207\t## Open items for later (the things to think about with a fresh head)\n208\t\n209\tThese are settled enough in shape that implementation can\n210\tstart, but the exact mechanics want one more pass:\n211\t\n212\t1. **How exactly does \"empty spec → delete\" detection work?**\n213\t   The user's framing: \"we essentially clear the box's fields\n214\t   one by one until we reach the end and realize 'hey this box\n215\t   isn't attached to anything, doesn't have a function, and\n216\t   has a refcount of 0 — it's obviously meant to be deleted.'\"\n217\t   Two possible mechanics:\n218\t   - **Wholesale replace then check:** apply the entire new\n219\t     spec, then look at the resulting struct. If function\n220\t     pointer is null, no connections, no inputs — interpret as\n221\t     delete, swap graph slot to NULL instead of NEW.\n222\t   - **Explicit DELETE tag:** the utility-box decides up\n223\t     front based on spec content and pushes RING_DELETE\n224\t     (separate tag from RING_RECONFIGURE) so the mutator\n225\t     doesn't have to do the emptiness check after applying.\n226\t   Lean toward the explicit tag — keeps the mutator's\n227\t   decision simple — but the implicit \"fields-equal-zero\n228\t   means delete\" is closer to the user's stated intuition.\n229\t\n230\t2. **For CREATE specifically: where does the wire endpoint\n231\t   point?** Reconfigure and delete both target an existing\n232\t   box (the wire endpoint). Create has no existing target —\n233\t   the box is born when the spec is applied. Today's\n234\t   BOX_CREATE_BOX emits the new id as a string output, and\n235\t   downstream consumers receive that string normally. We\n236\t   should keep that shape: for create, the output wire\n237\t   carries the new id as a value, not a control message.\n238\t\n239\t3. **Should the wire endpoint be the only target indicator?**\n240\t   The user leaned both ways in the discussion: \"we shouldn't\n241\t   take an ID as input at all\" AND \"lean option B\" (content-\n242\t   driven by id field). These are in tension. Probably the\n243\t   right resolution:\n244\t   - For reconfigure/delete: wire endpoint is the only target;\n245\t     no id in spec needed.\n246\t   - For create: the spec's id field IS required (since\n247\t     there's no existing target to wire to). The output wire\n248\t     carries the new id.\n249\t   - If spec for reconfigure/delete includes an id, it must\n250\t     match the wire endpoint's box id (safety check).\n251\t\n252\t4. **Detecting \"reconfigure vs create\" from the spec / wire.**\n253\t   - If the wire endpoint exists → it's a reconfigure or delete\n254\t     (decided by spec content emptiness).\n255\t   - If there's no wire endpoint (or it points at something\n256\t     invalid) → it's a create.\n257\t   Mechanically: the utility-box, on fire, looks at where its\n258\t   output wire is going. If the endpoint exists, push the\n259\t   struct pointer with a control tag. If not, allocate a new\n260\t   graph slot, install, emit the new id.\n261\t\n262\t5. **Should the utility-box have a name change?** \"utility-\n263\t   box\" was the user's tentative rename. Alternatives floated:\n264\t   \"spec-applier\", \"box-control\", \"reconfigure-box.\" Lean\n265\t   utility-box because it absorbs three operations.\n266\t\n267\t## Suggested implementation steps (subject to open-item resolution)\n268\t\n269\t1. Add the gather flag and refcount to box_t in\n270\t   `src/010-graph-loader.h`.\n271\t2. Convert the graph's box storage from array-of-structs to\n272\t   array-of-pointers so the mutator can swap.\n273\t3. Refactor `parse_box_file` to accept an in-memory JSON\n274\t   buffer (the utility-box's invoke will call this on the\n275\t   spec it received).\n276\t4. Add RING_RECONFIGURE (and possibly RING_DELETE) as\n277\t   ordering-ring tag values in `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}`.\n278\t   Each input slot's existing ring system grows the new tag\n279\t   variants.\n280\t5. Wrap `dispatch_action`'s top with the gather-flag CAS;\n281\t   yarq on loss, continue on win.\n282\t6. Add the mutator path inside `dispatch_action`: pop the\n283\t   tagged cell, resolve slot mappings, swap the graph slot,\n284\t   release the flag, yarq.\n285\t7. Add the utility-box kind in `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}`\n286\t   and `src/012-dispatch.c`. Folds in 319f's BOX_CREATE_BOX\n287\t   behaviour for the create path; adds the reconfigure /\n288\t   delete paths.\n289\t8. Add the JSONL events (box_reconfigure, reconfigure_discard,\n290\t   box_delete).\n291\t9. Fixtures under `tests/maps/`:\n292\t   - `320-utility-create` — regression that the unified\n293\t     utility-box still creates (matches 319f's existing\n294\t     create test).\n295\t   - `320-utility-reconfigure-fn` — fires once with old fn,\n296\t     reconfigures, fires again with new fn.\n297\t   - `320-utility-reconfigure-port-shape` — adds a port,\n298\t     verifies new slot allocated.\n299\t   - `320-utility-reconfigure-kind` — flips BOX_CALL to\n300\t     BOX_WRITE.\n301\t   - `320-utility-delete` — deletes a box; downstream\n302\t     gracefully handles the now-gone target.\n303\t   - `320-utility-contended` — two utility-boxes race on the\n304\t     same target; both reconfigures apply in arrival order.\n305\t\n306\t## Relevant files\n307\t\n308\t- `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}` — extend ordering ring tag with\n309\t  RING_RECONFIGURE (and possibly RING_DELETE).\n310\t- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — gather flag + refcount on\n311\t  box_t; parse_box_file refactor; graph array → array of\n312\t  pointers.\n313\t- `src/012-dispatch.c` — gather lock acquire/release; mutator\n314\t  path; utility-box kind dispatch.\n315\t- `src/013-jsonl-events.{c,h}` / `src/014-event-queue.{c,h}` —\n316\t  three new event types.\n317\t\n318\t## Design history\n319\t\n320\tTwo prior shapes were considered and discarded:\n321\t\n322\t1. **Per-port third ring with three-state flag (0/1/2)** — the\n323\t   original sketch. Discarded once we recognised the graph\n324\t   slot pointer can act as the liveness indicator, eliminating\n325\t   the third state.\n326\t2. **Per-box control queue + language-bridge `apply_box`\n327\t   function** — proposed as a way to keep control messages\n328\t   separate from data messages. Discarded once the user\n329\t   clarified the architectural principle: graph mutation is a\n330\t   box-kind operation, not a library function. The control\n331\t   queue was a layer mismatch — the existing input slots with\n332\t   the RING_RECONFIGURE tag (atomic gather makes peek-then-pop\n333\t   safe inside the lock) work fine.\n334\t\n335\tThe YARQ name (yield-and-requeue) came from the user's\n336\tphrasing: \"it's essentially 'hey we're not ready yet, put\n337\tyourself back at the beginning of the queue please.'\"\n338\t\n339\tThe unification of create / reconfigure / delete into one\n340\tutility-box kind came from the user's observation: \"What if we\n341\tmade the 'create-a-box' box take the same type of struct as the\n342\treconfigure process?\" The architectural principle that drove\n343\tthe final shape: graph mutation is a box-kind, the wire\n344\tendpoint is the target, and the runtime is the substrate, not\n345\tthe library.\n346\t"
      }]

      "content":"# 320 — Box reconfigure via the utility-box kind\n\n## Status\nopen · design substantially settled; a few naming / mechanical\ndetails still need a fresh-morning pass (see \"Open items for\nlater\" at the bottom)\n\n## Current behavior\n\nA box's identity is frozen at graph-load. The loader parses\n`boxes/<id>.json` once, fills in every field — kind, language,\nfunction pointer, input port list, routing shape, outgoing\nconnections — and the dispatch layer reads that record on every\nfire. Nothing in the running program changes a box's record.\n\nIssue 319f already added a language-agnostic way to **create**\nnew boxes at runtime: the BOX_CREATE_BOX kind. A box of that\nkind takes a JSON spec on a `spec` input port; when it fires, it\nparses the spec, allocates the new box at a fresh graph slot,\nand emits the new id as a string on its output wire. Downstream\nconsumers can take the id and wire the new box up further (via\nBOX_CONNECT).\n\nThis issue extends that pattern to **reconfigure** and\n**delete** — also via a box kind, not via library functions in\nthe language bridges. The new mechanism is the utility-box: a\nsingle box kind that subsumes create / reconfigure / delete by\ninspecting the spec it receives.\n\n## Architectural ground rules (from the design discussion)\n\n1. **Graph mutation is a box-kind operation, not a language-\n   bridge function.** Users express mutations by placing\n   utility-boxes in their maps and wiring them, not by writing\n   code that imports the runtime as a library. The runtime is\n   the substrate; users express through it.\n2. **Manual JSON construction is supported but not assisted.**\n   A user who wants to build a long-running Lua box that\n   accumulates JSON for its own future reconfigure can do so —\n   the manual path works as long as the bytes end up on a wire\n   that feeds a utility-box. We don't provide convenience\n   helpers for this path; the box-and-wire path is the intended\n   one.\n3. **The wire endpoint is the target.** When a utility-box's\n   output wire points at box X's input port, that wire IS the\n   declaration of \"this utility-box operates on box X.\" No\n   separate target_id input.\n4. **One unified box kind.** create / reconfigure / delete are\n   all the same kind, dispatched by the spec's content. Folds\n   in 319f's BOX_CREATE_BOX.\n5. **Reconfigures ride the regular input slots, tagged with\n   RING_RECONFIGURE.** No separate control queue. The gather\n   lock makes peek-then-pop honest within the lock, so the\n   peek-vs-pop race that would have plagued an unsynchronised\n   design doesn't appear.\n\n## The five structural pieces\n\n### 1. The utility-box kind\n\nOne box kind absorbs 319f's BOX_CREATE_BOX. The behaviour\ndepends on the spec's content:\n\n- **Spec describes a box with no existing target** (or target\n  doesn't exist in the graph): CREATE. Allocate at a fresh\n  graph slot. Emit the new id as a string on the output wire.\n- **Spec describes a box with an existing target id** (the\n  wire's endpoint matches an existing box): RECONFIGURE.\n  Build a replacement box struct. Push the struct pointer to\n  the target box's input slot (whichever port the wire ends\n  at), tagged RING_RECONFIGURE.\n- **Spec is empty / minimal** (no fields, no function): DELETE\n  signal. Push a delete-marker to the target box's input slot,\n  tagged RING_DELETE (or maybe just RING_RECONFIGURE with a\n  null struct — see open items).\n\n### 2. The gather flag\n\nEach box record carries one atomic int — the gather flag — with\ntwo states:\n\n- **0 (free):** any worker may attempt to enter.\n- **1 (held):** exactly one worker is currently in the gather\n  section.\n\nWorkers entering a box CAS from 0 to 1. Win → enter gather.\nLose → yield-and-requeue (push task back to pool tail and\nreturn). No spinning, no blocking. Per-box, so workers on\ndifferent boxes don't contend.\n\n### 3. The graph slot as liveness indicator\n\nThe graph stores boxes as an array of pointers, one per box id.\n`graph_box(graph, id)` returns the current pointer. The mutator\natomically swaps that pointer from OLD to NEW. Workers that\nLATER look up the same id get NEW; workers mid-fire on OLD\nfinish on OLD.\n\nA box is **live** when the graph slot points at it. **Superseded**\nwhen the graph slot points at something else (or NULL, for\ndelete). The two-state flag doesn't need a third \"redirect\"\nstate because the graph slot itself is the source of truth for\n\"is this still the current box.\"\n\n### 4. The reference count\n\nEach box record has an atomic refcount. Workers increment at\nthe top of their fire (just after resolving the pointer through\nthe graph slot) and decrement at the bottom. The mutator\nincrements when it becomes the mutator, decrements after the\nswap.\n\nFreeable when BOTH:\n- Graph slot doesn't point at this box (superseded), AND\n- Refcount has dropped to zero.\n\nWhoever decrements to zero AND observes the graph-slot\nmismatch frees the box. Mutator's \"hold a +1\" discipline\nensures the refcount only reaches zero AFTER the mutator\nfinishes its work.\n\n### 5. The reconfigure tag on input slots\n\nEach input slot's ordering ring grows a RING_RECONFIGURE value\nalongside the existing RING_NATIVE and RING_JSON. When a worker\nenters gather and peeks each input port's ordering ring, a\nRING_RECONFIGURE tag at any port's head means \"this is a\ncontrol message, not a value.\" The cell holds a pointer to a\npre-built box_t struct (built by the utility-box during its\nfire, in the utility-box's worker thread — no parsing happens\ninside the target's mutator path).\n\n## The worker arrival flow\n\n```\n1. look up the graph slot for X → get current box pointer (B).\n2. CAS B's gather flag from 0 to 1.\n   - lose: yarq this task. exit.\n   - win: continue.\n3. increment B's refcount.\n4. peek each of B's input ports' ordering rings.\n5. branch on what's there:\n     all heads are values:\n        pop one ordering message + value from each port.\n        release gather flag.\n        call spec's invoke with the gathered values.\n        push outputs to downstream slots per B's connections.\n        decrement B's refcount; free if it qualifies.\n\n     any head is RING_RECONFIGURE:\n        pop the struct pointer from that port.\n        run mutator path (see below).\n\n     some ports empty:\n        don't pop anything. release gather flag. exit.\n        the pool will respawn this box when more values arrive.\n```\n\n## The mutator flow\n\n```\n1. with B's gather flag held and refcount +1:\n2. pop the replacement struct pointer (call it NEW) from the\n   RING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell.\n3. resolve NEW's input_slot_ids by port-name matching:\n     for each port in NEW:\n       if same name exists in B (= OLD): copy that slot id.\n       else: allocate a fresh slot.\n4. record OLD's abandoned slots (in OLD but not in NEW). They\n   get released when OLD is freed; remaining queued values\n   emit a discard JSONL event per slot.\n5. atomically swap the graph slot for the box id from OLD to NEW.\n6. release B's gather flag.\n7. yarq this task. (queued values on B's slots — still there,\n   slot store didn't change — get fired under the new shape\n   when the requeued task runs.)\n8. decrement OLD's refcount; free OLD + abandoned slots if 0.\n```\n\nThe yarq in step 7 is load-bearing — without it, queued values\non B's input slots sit unprocessed until the next producer\npush.\n\n## Slot diffing by port name\n\nSame algorithm as UI framework tree diffing: match by stable\nidentifier (port name), only-in-old gets abandoned, only-in-new\ngets freshly allocated, in-both keeps its slot id (and its\nqueued values).\n\nRenames are NOT auto-detected — \"x\" → \"x_renamed\" looks like\n\"delete x, create x_renamed.\" Any queued values on \"x\" get\ndropped to JSONL. Value-preserving rename via explicit\n`{\"rename\":[...]}` field is a possible future addition.\n\n## JSONL events\n\n- `box_reconfigure` — fires after the swap. Carries box id,\n  post-apply spec (re-serialised from the new struct), task id,\n  worker idx. Always emitted.\n- `reconfigure_discard` — fires per abandoned port with leftover\n  values. Carries box id, port name, count of dropped values,\n  first value bytes (up to 256). Always emitted.\n- `box_delete` — fires when the spec resolves to a delete.\n  Carries box id, task id, worker idx. Always emitted.\n\n## Open items for later (the things to think about with a fresh head)\n\nThese are settled enough in shape that implementation can\nstart, but the exact mechanics want one more pass:\n\n1. **How exactly does \"empty spec → delete\" detection work?**\n   The user's framing: \"we essentially clear the box's fields\n   one by one until we reach the end and realize 'hey this box\n   isn't attached to anything, doesn't have a function, and\n   has a refcount of 0 — it's obviously meant to be deleted.'\"\n   Two possible mechanics:\n   - **Wholesale replace then check:** apply the entire new\n     spec, then look at the resulting struct. If function\n     pointer is null, no connections, no inputs — interpret as\n     delete, swap graph slot to NULL instead of NEW.\n   - **Explicit DELETE tag:** the utility-box decides up\n     front based on spec content and pushes RING_DELETE\n     (separate tag from RING_RECONFIGURE) so the mutator\n     doesn't have to do the emptiness check after applying.\n   Lean toward the explicit tag — keeps the mutator's\n   decision simple — but the implicit \"fields-equal-zero\n   means delete\" is closer to the user's stated intuition.\n\n2. **For CREATE specifically: where does the wire endpoint\n   point?** Reconfigure and delete both target an existing\n   box (the wire endpoint). Create has no existing target —\n   the box is born when the spec is applied. Today's\n   BOX_CREATE_BOX emits the new id as a string output, and\n   downstream consumers receive that string normally. We\n   should keep that shape: for create, the output wire\n   carries the new id as a value, not a control message.\n\n3. **Should the wire endpoint be the only target indicator?**\n   The user leaned both ways in the discussion: \"we shouldn't\n   take an ID as input at all\" AND \"lean option B\" (content-\n   driven by id field). These are in tension. Probably the\n   right resolution:\n   - For reconfigure/delete: wire endpoint is the only target;\n     no id in spec needed.\n   - For create: the spec's id field IS required (since\n     there's no existing target to wire to). The output wire\n     carries the new id.\n   - If spec for reconfigure/delete includes an id, it must\n     match the wire endpoint's box id (safety check).\n\n4. **Detecting \"reconfigure vs create\" from the spec / wire.**\n   - If the wire endpoint exists → it's a reconfigure or delete\n     (decided by spec content emptiness).\n   - If there's no wire endpoint (or it points at something\n     invalid) → it's a create.\n   Mechanically: the utility-box, on fire, looks at where its\n   output wire is going. If the endpoint exists, push the\n   struct pointer with a control tag. If not, allocate a new\n   graph slot, install, emit the new id.\n\n5. **Should the utility-box have a name change?** \"utility-\n   box\" was the user's tentative rename. Alternatives floated:\n   \"spec-applier\", \"box-control\", \"reconfigure-box.\" Lean\n   utility-box because it absorbs three operations.\n\n## Suggested implementation steps (subject to open-item resolution)\n\n1. Add the gather flag and refcount to box_t in\n   `src/010-graph-loader.h`.\n2. Convert the graph's box storage from array-of-structs to\n   array-of-pointers so the mutator can swap.\n3. Refactor `parse_box_file` to accept an in-memory JSON\n   buffer (the utility-box's invoke will call this on the\n   spec it received).\n4. Add RING_RECONFIGURE (and possibly RING_DELETE) as\n   ordering-ring tag values in `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}`.\n   Each input slot's existing ring system grows the new tag\n   variants.\n5. Wrap `dispatch_action`'s top with the gather-flag CAS;\n   yarq on loss, continue on win.\n6. Add the mutator path inside `dispatch_action`: pop the\n   tagged cell, resolve slot mappings, swap the graph slot,\n   release the flag, yarq.\n7. Add the utility-box kind in `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}`\n   and `src/012-dispatch.c`. Folds in 319f's BOX_CREATE_BOX\n   behaviour for the create path; adds the reconfigure /\n   delete paths.\n8. Add the JSONL events (box_reconfigure, reconfigure_discard,\n   box_delete).\n9. Fixtures under `tests/maps/`:\n   - `320-utility-create` — regression that the unified\n     utility-box still creates (matches 319f's existing\n     create test).\n   - `320-utility-reconfigure-fn` — fires once with old fn,\n     reconfigures, fires again with new fn.\n   - `320-utility-reconfigure-port-shape` — adds a port,\n     verifies new slot allocated.\n   - `320-utility-reconfigure-kind` — flips BOX_CALL to\n     BOX_WRITE.\n   - `320-utility-delete` — deletes a box; downstream\n     gracefully handles the now-gone target.\n   - `320-utility-contended` — two utility-boxes race on the\n     same target; both reconfigures apply in arrival order.\n\n## Relevant files\n\n- `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}` — extend ordering ring tag with\n  RING_RECONFIGURE (and possibly RING_DELETE).\n- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — gather flag + refcount on\n  box_t; parse_box_file refactor; graph array → array of\n  pointers.\n- `src/012-dispatch.c` — gather lock acquire/release; mutator\n  path; utility-box kind dispatch.\n- `src/013-jsonl-events.{c,h}` / `src/014-event-queue.{c,h}` —\n  three new event types.\n\n## Design history\n\nTwo prior shapes were considered and discarded:\n\n1. **Per-port third ring with three-state flag (0/1/2)** — the\n   original sketch. Discarded once we recognised the graph\n   slot pointer can act as the liveness indicator, eliminating\n   the third state.\n2. **Per-box control queue + language-bridge `apply_box`\n   function** — proposed as a way to keep control messages\n   separate from data messages. Discarded once the user\n   clarified the architectural principle: graph mutation is a\n   box-kind operation, not a library function. The control\n   queue was a layer mismatch — the existing input slots with\n   the RING_RECONFIGURE tag (atomic gather makes peek-then-pop\n   safe inside the lock) work fine.\n\nThe YARQ name (yield-and-requeue) came from the user's\nphrasing: \"it's essentially 'hey we're not ready yet, put\nyourself back at the beginning of the queue please.'\"\n\nThe unification of create / reconfigure / delete into one\nutility-box kind came from the user's observation: \"What if we\nmade the 'create-a-box' box take the same type of struct as the\nreconfigure process?\" The architectural principle that drove\nthe final shape: graph mutation is a box-kind, the wire\nendpoint is the target, and the runtime is the substrate, not\nthe library.\n",
      "numLines":346
    }
  },

The way it\n11\tdoes this today is a special-case atomic dance directly on the\n12\tbox's `connections` field: allocate a new connections array,\n13\tcopy the old entries, append the new entry, atomic-store the\n14\tpointer, then atomic-store the new count. Readers that walk\n15\tthe connections array use the order \"read count first, then\n16\tpointer\" so they always observe either the old pair or the new\n17\tpair, never a torn mix.\n18\t\n19\tThis works because the connections array is append-only —\n20\truntime_connect only ever adds, never removes or reorders.\n21\tThat's the reason the staggered-publish pattern doesn't break\n22\texisting readers.\n23\t\n24\tOnce issue 320 lands, the runtime has a more general mechanism:\n25\tbuild a fully replacement box struct, push its pointer to the\n26\ttarget's control queue, let the next worker visit do the\n27\tatomic graph-slot swap. This mechanism is strictly more\n28\tpowerful than the connections-only dance — it can change\n29\tanything about the box, not just append to one field.\n30\t\n31\t## Intended behavior\n32\t\n33\tRetire the special-case copy-and-publish on the connections\n34\tfield. Re-implement `runtime_connect` as a thin wrapper over\n35\t`runtime_apply_box_spec`:\n36\t\n37\t1. Look up the current box for the target id.\n38\t2. Build a new box struct that is a copy of the current one,\n39\t   with the new connection appended to its connections array.\n40\t3. Call `runtime_apply_box_spec(target_id, new_spec_json)` (or\n41\t   the underlying push-pointer-to-control-queue equivalent).\n42\t\n43\tThe atomic discipline collapses to one path. The connections\n44\tfield on `box_t` can become a plain pointer + plain int (no\n45\t`_Atomic` qualifiers needed) because the only way to mutate it\n46\tis via the box-swap mechanism, which never modifies a live box\n47\tin place.\n48\t\n49\t## Why bother\n50\t\n51\t- **One mutation discipline instead of two.** Every change to\n52\t  a box's identity — adding a connection, changing the function\n53\t  pointer, renaming a port, deleting the box — uses the same\n54\t  build-new-and-swap pattern. Less code to maintain, fewer\n55\t  edge cases to learn.\n56\t- **The connections-only dance is structurally fragile.** It\n57\t  relies on append-only semantics that nobody enforces at the\n58\t  type level. A future change that wanted to remove or reorder\n59\t  connections would silently introduce torn-read bugs. Removing\n60\t  the path removes the trap.\n61\t- **Smaller box_t.** The `_Atomic` qualifiers on\n62\t  `n_connections` and `connections` can come off. Plain int +\n63\t  plain pointer is fine because mutation only happens during\n64\t  the build-new step, before the new box is published.\n65\t\n66\t## Suggested implementation steps\n67\t\n68\t1. **Re-implement `runtime_connect` as a wrapper over\n69\t   `runtime_apply_box_spec`.** Caller still sees the same\n70\t   signature; internals route through the box-swap path.\n71\t2. **Remove the `_Atomic` qualifiers on `n_connections` and\n72\t   `connections` in `src/010-graph-loader.h`.** Plain types\n73\t   suffice now.\n74\t3. **Remove the copy-and-publish helper in\n75\t   `src/010-graph-loader.c`** that runtime_connect used to\n76\t   call.\n77\t4. **Verify the existing 319d fixture (`319d-runtime-create`,\n78\t   `319e-c-create`, etc.) still passes under the new path.**\n79\t   The behavioural contract for runtime_connect is unchanged;\n80\t   the internal mechanism changes.\n81\t\n82\t## Relevant files\n83\t\n84\t- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — drop atomic qualifiers; drop\n85\t  copy-and-publish helper.\n86\t- `src/018-runtime-builtins.{c,h}` — rewrite runtime_connect\n87\t  to call into runtime_apply_box_spec.\n88\t- Existing 319d/319e/319f fixtures act as the regression\n89\t  guard; no new fixtures needed.\n90\t\n91\t## Open questions\n92\t\n93\t- **Does the language bridges need any change?** Lua's\n94\t  `soramech.connect`, Bash's `soramech_connect`, C's\n95\t  `soramech_connect` all currently call straight into\n96\t  runtime_connect. If runtime_connect's signature stays the\n97\t  same, no bridge changes. Confirm during implementation.\n98\t"

      "content":"# 321 — Unify connections-array mutation through the 320 box-swap path\n\n## Status\nopen · waits on 320 landing first; design is straightforward\nonce the box-swap mechanism exists\n\n## Current behavior\n\nIssue 319d added the ability to attach new outgoing connections\nto an existing box at runtime via `runtime_connect`. The way it\ndoes this today is a special-case atomic dance directly on the\nbox's `connections` field: allocate a new connections array,\ncopy the old entries, append the new entry, atomic-store the\npointer, then atomic-store the new count. Readers that walk\nthe connections array use the order \"read count first, then\npointer\" so they always observe either the old pair or the new\npair, never a torn mix.\n\nThis works because the connections array is append-only —\nruntime_connect only ever adds, never removes or reorders.\nThat's the reason the staggered-publish pattern doesn't break\nexisting readers.\n\nOnce issue 320 lands, the runtime has a more general mechanism:\nbuild a fully replacement box struct, push its pointer to the\ntarget's control queue, let the next worker visit do the\natomic graph-slot swap. Re-implement `runtime_connect` as a thin wrapper over\n`runtime_apply_box_spec`:\n\n1. Look up the current box for the target id.\n2. Build a new box struct that is a copy of the current one,\n   with the new connection appended to its connections array.\n3. Call `runtime_apply_box_spec(target_id, new_spec_json)` (or\n   the underlying push-pointer-to-control-queue equivalent).\n\nThe atomic discipline collapses to one path. The connections\nfield on `box_t` can become a plain pointer + plain int (no\n`_Atomic` qualifiers needed) because the only way to mutate it\nis via the box-swap mechanism, which never modifies a live box\nin place.\n\n## Why bother\n\n- **One mutation discipline instead of two.** Every change to\n  a box's identity — adding a connection, changing the function\n  pointer, renaming a port, deleting the box — uses the same\n  build-new-and-swap pattern. Less code to maintain, fewer\n  edge cases to learn.\n- **The connections-only dance is structurally fragile.** It\n  relies on append-only semantics that nobody enforces at the\n  type level. Removing\n  the path removes the trap.\n- **Smaller box_t.** The `_Atomic` qualifiers on\n  `n_connections` and `connections` can come off. Plain int +\n  plain pointer is fine because mutation only happens during\n  the build-new step, before the new box is published.\n\n## Suggested implementation steps\n\n1. **Re-implement `runtime_connect` as a wrapper over\n   `runtime_apply_box_spec`.** Caller still sees the same\n   signature; internals route through the box-swap path.\n2. **Remove the `_Atomic` qualifiers on `n_connections` and\n   `connections` in `src/010-graph-loader.h`.** Plain types\n   suffice now.\n3. **Remove the copy-and-publish helper in\n   `src/010-graph-loader.c`** that runtime_connect used to\n   call.\n4. **Verify the existing 319d fixture (`319d-runtime-create`,\n   `319e-c-create`, etc.) still passes under the new path.**\n   The behavioural contract for runtime_connect is unchanged;\n   the internal mechanism changes.\n\n## Relevant files\n\n- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — drop atomic qualifiers; drop\n  copy-and-publish helper.\n- `src/018-runtime-builtins.{c,h}` — rewrite runtime_connect\n  to call into runtime_apply_box_spec.\n- Existing 319d/319e/319f fixtures act as the regression\n  guard; no new fixtures needed.\n\n## Open questions\n\n- **Does the language bridges need any change?** Lua's\n  `soramech.connect`, Bash's `soramech_connect`, C's\n  `soramech_connect` all currently call straight into\n  runtime_connect. ## [17]

```json
{
  "cwd":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech",
  "type":"user",
  "timestamp":"2026-05-25T15:39:49.790Z",
  "gitBranch":"master",
  "version":"2.1.121",
  "sessionId":"f841ede1-ab42-4757-bba0-385e56b84f84",
  "isSidechain":false,
  "message":{
    "role":"user",
    "content":[{
        "tool_use_id":"toolu_019C7fyzNHfRgmMLCV8V1jZK",
        "type":"tool_result",
        "content":"1\t# 322 — Box delete: the user-facing experience\n2\t\n3\t## Status\n4\topen · the underlying mechanism is part of 320; this issue\n5\tcovers the user-facing API and the explicit-vs-implicit-deletion\n6\tdesign decision\n7\t\n8\t## Current behavior\n9\t\n10\tOnce issue 320 lands, the runtime has a unified\n11\t`runtime_apply_box_spec(target_id, spec_json)` entry point that\n12\thandles create / reconfigure / delete based on the spec's\n13\tcontent. The delete path is: install NULL at the graph slot\n14\tvia the same supersession mechanism, let the refcount drive\n15\tthe old box's eventual free.\n16\t\n17\tWhat 320 does NOT cover, and what this issue is for:\n18\t\n19\t- The user-facing spec shape that triggers deletion.\n20\t- A convenience wrapper for the common case.\n21\t- Validation that catches \"I forgot to fill in fields\"\n22\t  accidents from masquerading as deletes.\n23\t- The downstream effects when consumers wired to a deleted box\n24\t  try to push values into the now-gone graph slot.\n25\t\n26\t## Why deletion needs more than just \"any minimum spec\"\n27\t\n28\tThe natural shortcut would be: \"if the spec only has an id and\n29\tno other fields, treat it as a delete.\" Compact and elegant.\n30\t\n31\tBut it's also dangerous: a user who forgets to populate fields\n32\tin their reconfigure spec accidentally deletes the box. Defaults\n33\tshould fail-closed (do nothing surprising) rather than fail-open\n34\t(do something destructive). The minimum-spec-means-delete shape\n35\tfails open.\n36\t\n37\tThe safer alternative is an explicit marker in the spec:\n38\t\n39\t```json\n40\t{\"action\": \"delete\", \"id\": \"target_box_id\"}\n41\t```\n42\t\n43\tThe runtime parses the spec, sees `action == \"delete\"`, and\n44\troutes through the delete path. Specs without an explicit\n45\t`action` field default to create-or-reconfigure (the inferred\n46\taction based on whether target_id exists). Explicit beats\n47\timplicit; destructive operations are always explicit.\n48\t\n49\t## Convenience wrapper\n50\t\n51\tFor the common case where a user just wants to delete a box\n52\tthey know the id of, add a sugar function:\n53\t\n54\t```c\n55\tint runtime_destroy_box(const char *target_id, char **err_out);\n56\t```\n57\t\n58\tInternally, this constructs the delete spec JSON and calls\n59\t`runtime_apply_box_spec`. Same code path, friendlier signature.\n60\t\n61\tSame shape per language bridge:\n62\t\n63\t- Lua: `soramech.destroy_box(target_id)`\n64\t- C:   `soramech_destroy_box(target_id, err_buf, err_cap)`\n65\t- Bash: `soramech_destroy_box \"$target_id\"`\n66\t\n67\t## Downstream effects of deletion\n68\t\n69\tA box X is deleted. Some other box Y has a connection pointing\n70\tat X. Y fires, produces an output, attempts to push to X's\n71\tinput slots — but X is gone from the graph slot.\n72\t\n73\tTwo possible behaviours:\n74\t\n75\t- **Silent drop:** the push fails because the target lookup\n76\t  returns NULL. Producer continues; output is lost. A push\n77\t  event with `result=target-gone` records the loss in the\n78\t  JSONL transcript.\n79\t- **Hard error:** the push fails and the producer's fire is\n80\t  treated as a failure. The run aborts (or the producer's\n81\t  spec returns an error code).\n82\t\n83\tThe user's stated preference throughout the project is \"errors\n84\tover fallbacks.\" So hard error is the strict reading. But\n85\tdeletion is a deliberate user action — if they deleted X, they\n86\tprobably know Y depended on it. Hard-error-on-every-push would\n87\tmean a single deleted leaf-box could crash the entire run.\n88\t\n89\tA middle path: silent drop by default, with the JSONL event\n90\tmaking the loss observable, AND a runtime knob (env var or\n91\tspec field) to upgrade the drop to a hard error for users who\n92\twant strict behaviour.\n93\t\n94\t## Suggested implementation steps\n95\t\n96\t1. **Define the spec shape for delete.** `{\"action\": \"delete\",\n97\t   \"id\": \"...\"}`. Document that the `action` field defaults to\n98\t   \"apply\" (which means create-or-reconfigure based on target\n99\t   existence) and the only other recognised value is \"delete\".\n100\t2. **Implement the delete path inside\n101\t   `runtime_apply_box_spec`.** Already part of 320's\n102\t   implementation; this is just adding the action dispatch.\n103\t3. **Add `runtime_destroy_box` as the sugar wrapper.** Three\n104\t   language bridges plus the C entry point.\n105\t4. **Add the push-target-gone JSONL event.** Same shape as\n106\t   other push events; `result=target-gone` is the new value.\n107\t5. **Add an opt-in strict mode** for users who want push-to-\n108\t   deleted-box to hard-error rather than silent-drop. Env var\n109\t   `SORAMECH_STRICT_DELETE=1` or per-call flag, TBD.\n110\t6. **Fixtures:**\n111\t   - `tests/maps/322-delete-then-orphaned-push` — A box X is\n112\t     created; B is wired to X; X is deleted; B fires once more;\n113\t     the push fails with `result=target-gone` in the JSONL.\n114\t   - `tests/maps/322-delete-and-recreate` — A box X is created,\n115\t     deleted, then created again with the same id and different\n116\t     behaviour. The recreate succeeds at the now-NULL graph\n117\t     slot; subsequent fires use the new behaviour.\n118\t\n119\t## Relevant files\n120\t\n121\t- `src/018-runtime-builtins.{c,h}` — `runtime_destroy_box`\n122\t  sugar wrapper.\n123\t- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — `parse_box_file` extended to\n124\t  recognise the `action` field on the spec.\n125\t- `src/013-jsonl-events.{c,h}` / `src/014-event-queue.{c,h}` —\n126\t  `target-gone` as a new push result.\n127\t- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c` —\n128\t  one new bridge function per language for `destroy_box`.\n129\t\n130\t## Open questions\n131\t\n132\t- **Strict-mode default.** Should `SORAMECH_STRICT_DELETE=0`\n133\t  (silent drop default) or `=1` (hard error default) be the\n134\t  shipped default? Silent drop matches \"don't crash the run on\n135\t  a leaf-box deletion\"; hard error matches \"errors over\n136\t  fallbacks.\" Lean toward silent-drop-by-default + JSONL event\n137\t  + opt-in strict; revisit if a project disagrees.\n138\t- **Implicit recreation.** A delete followed by a create at\n139\t  the same id — should this work seamlessly, or require an\n140\t  explicit timing barrier? My instinct: works seamlessly. The\n141\t  graph slot transitions NULL → new-box-pointer atomically;\n142\t  any worker that arrives at the in-between state (graph slot\n143\t  is NULL) just exits gracefully.\n144\t"
