## [97]

```json
{
  "cwd":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech",
  "type":"assistant",
  "timestamp":"2026-05-25T15:45:24.353Z",
  "version":"2.1.121",
  "isSidechain":false,
  "message":{
    "model":"claude-opus-4-7",
    "stop_reason":"tool_use",
    "id":"msg_01Gp5pM7A2xRw7ZDXdqknTgC",
    "role":"assistant",
    "content":[{
        "name":"Write",
        "input":{
          "content":"# 320 / 321 / 322 — planning notes\n\nA planning document, not a spec. Stored at /tmp/ during the\ndesign conversation; rewrite into /docs/ once the open\nquestions at the bottom are resolved.\n\nThe three issues form one feature with one shared mechanism.\nThe piece that does the work is 320 (the box-swap path\nthrough a unified utility-box kind). 321 retires the\nexisting connections-append dance by routing it through the\nsame swap. 322 is the user-facing shape and the policy\nchoices around delete.\n\nThis document tries to make every atomic interaction and\nevery byte that crosses a thread boundary explicit.\n\n---\n\n## Part A — the shape, in cleartext\n\nA running graph today is a directed bag of boxes whose\nidentity is frozen at load. The graph's box index is an\narray of pointers, one entry per box id. The box record\nbehind each pointer holds the function, the input port\ndeclarations, the routing kind, and the outgoing\nconnections.\n\nThe change: that per-id pointer becomes the only handle\nthat's allowed to mutate, and the mutation is always the\nsame shape — install a fully-built replacement box at the\nslot, retire the old. Three user-visible operations\ncollapse onto that one mechanism:\n\n- **create**: no existing box at the id; the slot is fresh.\n  The replacement IS the new box.\n- **reconfigure**: an existing box; the replacement carries\n  the same id, possibly a different function, possibly\n  different ports, possibly different connections.\n- **delete**: an existing box; the replacement is NULL.\n\nAll three reach the runtime as JSON specs on the input wire\nof a single box kind — the utility-box. The utility-box\nreads its `spec` input port, parses, decides which of the\nthree operations the spec describes, and acts.\n\nThe wire endpoint of the utility-box's output is the\ntarget. There is no `target_id` input. \"This utility-box\noperates on box X\" means \"this utility-box's output wire\nends at one of X's input ports.\"\n\n---\n\n## Part B — atomic interactions, enumerated\n\nEvery shared piece of state across threads, what touches it,\nwhat ordering guarantees we depend on, and what the\ninvariant is.\n\n### B1. The graph slot pointer\n\n**Storage.** `graph_t::boxes` is `_Atomic(box_t **)`, an\narray of box pointers. Each entry `boxes[i]` is the per-id\nslot. Today the array itself can grow (copy-and-publish\nunder a graph mutex); the per-id pointer is set once at\nload and never changes.\n\n**Change.** The per-id pointer becomes mutable. A mutator\natomic-stores a replacement pointer at `boxes[i]`. Readers\natomic-load it.\n\n**The atomic op.**\n\n- writer: `atomic_store_explicit(&boxes[i], NEW, memory_order_release)`\n- reader: `atomic_load_explicit(&boxes[i], memory_order_acquire)`\n\nThe release/acquire pair ensures any reader who observes\nNEW also observes everything the mutator wrote into NEW's\nfields before publishing.\n\nNULL is a valid value (it means \"deleted\"). Readers must\ntolerate NULL gracefully.\n\n**Invariant.** \"The current box for id i is whatever\n`boxes[i]` points at right now.\" Workers who looked up\nboxes[i] before the swap and got OLD finish their work on\nOLD; they never re-resolve mid-task.\n\n**Open mechanical detail.** The per-id storage is today a\nsingle pointer per slot. We need to either keep it that way\n(an array of `_Atomic(box_t *)`) or change the surrounding\ninfrastructure to allow per-id atomic stores. The current\ncopy-and-publish growth path stores `box_t **` non-atomically\n(it's just the array, not the cells). The cells need\natomic-store semantics. Cleanest: make the array's cell\ntype `_Atomic(box_t *)`.\n\n### B2. The gather flag\n\n**Storage.** A new field on `box_t`, `_Atomic int gather_flag`.\nOne byte of meaning, two states:\n\n- 0 — free; any worker may try to enter.\n- 1 — held; exactly one worker is currently in the gather\n  section of this box.\n\n**Why per-box, not per-port.** A box's gather is a logical\noperation over all input ports of that box — peeking each\nordering ring head, deciding whether to pop or yield-and-\nrequeue, popping consistently. Multiple workers could\nin principle be at this same box, but only one can do the\npeek-then-pop without racing with another's pop. The\nper-box flag is the smallest serialisation that protects\nthat section. Different boxes don't contend.\n\n**The atomic op.**\n\n- enter: `atomic_compare_exchange_strong(&b->gather_flag, &expected=0, 1)`\n  with memory_order_acquire on success, memory_order_relaxed\n  on failure.\n- release: `atomic_store_explicit(&b->gather_flag, 0, memory_order_release)`.\n\n**Behaviour on contention.** Lose → yield-and-requeue\n(push the current task back to the pool's tail with\npriority 0, return without doing any work for this fire).\nNo spinning, no waiting. The runtime is busy elsewhere; come\nback later.\n\n**Invariant.** \"While a worker is between peek and pop on\nthis box's input ports, no other worker is doing the same\non this box.\"\n\n### B3. The reference count\n\n**Storage.** A new field on `box_t`, `_Atomic int refcount`.\n\n**Who increments.**\n\n- **Workers** that arrive at the box and have just won the\n  gather-flag CAS. They bump refcount to mark \"I am\n  currently executing on this box record.\" The bump happens\n  AFTER the CAS win so a freer that observes refcount == 0\n  also knows no worker is in mid-fire.\n- **Mutators** (the worker that is about to push a swap)\n  bump refcount before publishing the new pointer. This is\n  the \"+1 hold\" — it keeps the box alive long enough for\n  the mutator to finish even if every other worker has\n  already left.\n\n**Who decrements.**\n\n- Workers at the end of their fire, after pushing outputs.\n- The mutator after publishing the new pointer.\n\n**The atomic op.**\n\n- bump: `atomic_fetch_add_explicit(&b->refcount, 1, memory_order_acquire)`\n- drop: `atomic_fetch_sub_explicit(&b->refcount, 1, memory_order_release)`\n  returns previous value; if previous was 1, the dropper is\n  the last reference.\n\n**The free condition.** The last decrementer (previous\nvalue 1, so post-decrement is 0) checks the graph slot for\nthis box id. If `boxes[id]` no longer points at THIS box,\nthe dropper owns the free. If `boxes[id]` still points at\nTHIS box, do nothing — a future mutator will see this\nrefcount when it lands.\n\n**Why the mutator's +1 matters.** Sequence without it:\nmutator computes NEW, publishes at boxes[id], decrements\nrefcount, frees OLD. But another worker that was mid-fire\non OLD between the mutator's publish and the mutator's\ndecrement might still touch OLD's fields. The +1 hold\nprevents that — the mutator's hold keeps OLD alive across\nthe publish; the last drop is whichever worker leaves\nlast, and that drop happens-after every other touch.\n\n**Invariant.** \"A box is free-able exactly when (a) the\ngraph slot doesn't point at it AND (b) refcount has\ndropped to zero.\" Whoever observes both conditions owns\nthe free.\n\n### B4. The ordering ring's RING_RECONFIGURE tag\n\n**Storage.** Today the ordering ring on a dual-ring slot\nstores (which_ring, idx) tuples where which_ring is\nSLOT_RING_NATIVE (0) or SLOT_RING_JSON (1). The data lives\nin two separate native/JSON rings; the ordering ring\npreserves arrival order across both.\n\n**Change.** Add a third tag value, RING_RECONFIGURE (= 2).\nA cell tagged RING_RECONFIGURE carries the index of a\ncontrol-message slot whose payload is a `box_t *` (the\nfully-built replacement). For uniformity we either:\n\n- (a) push the box pointer through the existing native or\n  JSON ring as 8 bytes (the pointer value) and use the new\n  tag to mark it as \"interpret these bytes as a pointer,\n  not as a value\", OR\n- (b) add a third internal ring on the slot — a small\n  ring of `box_t *` pointers — and the tag means \"look at\n  ring C, not A or B\".\n\nOption (a) is cheaper (no new ring) and reuses the\nallocator. The cell capacity needs to be at least 8 bytes\nfor a 64-bit pointer; most slots already are. Option (b)\nis cleaner-typed but adds a third ring everywhere.\n\nLean (a) for the first slice. The control cell's payload\nis exactly `sizeof(box_t *)` bytes — the pointer to NEW.\n\n**The atomic op.** Pushing RING_RECONFIGURE is the same\nper-slot push under the existing per-slot spinlock; the\nordering tuple's atomicity comes from being written under\nthe same lock as the data ring's index advance.\n\n**Why peek-then-pop is safe.** The gather-flag held by a\nworker doesn't serialise pushes (pushes are per-slot, not\nper-box). But it serialises peeks and pops from the SAME\nworker, so if a worker peeks port P and observes\nRING_RECONFIGURE at the head, the subsequent pop on port P\nreturns that same RING_RECONFIGURE cell — because the per-\nslot ring is FIFO and the gather-lock-holding worker is\nthe only one popping this box's input slots right now.\nPushes after the peek append at the tail; they don't\ndisplace the head.\n\n**Invariant.** \"If a worker holds box B's gather flag and\npeeks port P's ordering ring head as X, the next pop on\nport P returns X.\" This is what makes the design lock-free\non the producer side and lock-protected only on the\nconsumer's gather section.\n\n### B5. The connections array (321's territory)\n\n**Today.** `box_t::n_connections` and `box_t::connections`\nare atomic. `box_add_connection` allocates a new array,\ncopies + appends, atomic-stores the array pointer first\n(release), then the count (release). Readers in dispatch\nload with acquire and see either the pre-append snapshot\nor the post-append snapshot, never a torn mix. The old\narray is parked on a stale list, freed at graph_destroy.\n\n**After 321.** The connections array is mutated only as\npart of building a NEW box for the swap. The NEW box's\nconnections are written before the swap publishes NEW at\nthe graph slot, so by the time any reader walks NEW's\nconnections array the writes have already happened-before\nthe slot's atomic-store. The atomic qualifiers come off;\nplain pointer + plain int is enough.\n\nThe stale list keeps existing function — old box records\nget parked when a swap happens, freed at refcount-zero +\ngraph-slot mismatch. The per-connection-array staleness\ncollapses into per-box staleness.\n\n### B6. Yield-and-requeue (YARQ)\n\n**Not an atomic primitive; a control-flow primitive.** A\nworker that loses the gather CAS, or a mutator that has\njust published the swap, calls back into\n`dispatch_spawn` with the same box id and priority 0.\nThe task lands at the tail of the pool's queue.\n\nThe mutator's YARQ in step 7 is load-bearing for a subtle\nreason. Queued values on the box's input slots survive the\nswap (the slots are owned per-port, identified by slot id\nwhich the new box record may or may not have inherited\ndepending on port-name match). Without the YARQ, those\nqueued values just sit there until the next producer\npushes — which might be never. The YARQ schedules a fire\non the new box that drains them.\n\n---\n\n## Part C — the values on the wires\n\n### C1. What the utility-box receives on its `spec` input\n\nA single string-shaped value, JSON object syntax. Same\nshape as the on-disk `boxes/<id>.json` file. Three rough\nflavours:\n\n**Create:**\n\n```json\n{\n  \"kind\": \"call\",\n  \"lang\": \"lua\",\n  \"ref\": \"my_fn.lua\",\n  \"fn\": \"do_thing\",\n  \"inputs\": [{\"name\": \"x\", \"type\": \"string\"}],\n  \"routing\": {\"kind\": \"plain\"}\n}\n```\n\nNo id; the runtime auto-generates. Alternatively an\nexplicit `\"id\": \"...\"` can be supplied. No connections —\nthe utility-box's output wire delivers the new id to a\ndownstream consumer (typically a connect box), and that\nconsumer wires it up.\n\n**Reconfigure (implicit, the inferred form):**\n\n```json\n{\n  \"kind\": \"call\",\n  \"lang\": \"lua\",\n  \"ref\": \"my_fn.lua\",\n  \"fn\": \"do_thing_v2\",\n  \"inputs\": [{\"name\": \"x\", \"type\": \"string\"}]\n}\n```\n\nNo `action` field — the utility-box infers from the wire\nendpoint. The wire ends at box X's `whatever` port → the\noperation targets X.\n\n**Delete (explicit, per 322):**\n\n```json\n{ \"action\": \"delete\" }\n```\n\nOr with a redundant id check:\n\n```json\n{ \"action\": \"delete\", \"id\": \"X\" }\n```\n\nIf `id` is present, the runtime checks it matches the wire\nendpoint and errors out on mismatch — a safety check\nagainst accidentally targeting the wrong box.\n\n### C2. What the utility-box emits on its output\n\nDepends on which operation it dispatched:\n\n- **create**: emits the new box's id as a string. Length =\n  strlen(id). Same shape as today's BOX_CREATE_BOX output.\n  Downstream consumers receive an id and can build connect\n  specs that reference it.\n- **reconfigure**: emits the literal string \"true\" (or\n  similar success marker) on the side-effect output. The\n  primary effect is the swap; the output is just for chain-\n  ability.\n- **delete**: emits \"true\" on the side-effect output.\n\nThe wire from the utility-box's output to the target's\ninput port is the OPERATION POINTER — the bytes on it are\nnot what triggers the operation, the act of pushing them\nthrough this particular wire is. For reconfigure and\ndelete, the bytes are arbitrary (we emit \"true\" for\nchainability; the target ignores them and processes the\nRING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell that was pushed alongside).\n\nWait — this is the bit that needs precision. Two possible\nshapes:\n\n- **Single channel:** the utility-box pushes the\n  RING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell through its output wire.\n  That tagged cell carries the `box_t *` to NEW. The\n  target observes a RING_RECONFIGURE at the port the wire\n  ends at and goes into the mutator branch.\n- **Side channel:** the utility-box looks up the target\n  by walking its own output wire, builds NEW, pushes the\n  RING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell DIRECTLY to the target's\n  slot (not through its own output ring at all), and then\n  separately pushes \"true\" through its output for the\n  visible side effect.\n\nThe single-channel form is cleaner — one push, one wire,\none target — and matches the issue's stated mechanism\n(\"push to the target box's input slot\"). The side-channel\nform lets the utility-box's output also drive downstream\nchains for free; but that's a marginal convenience versus\nthe conceptual cost of \"the wire is a control message in\ndisguise.\"\n\n**Lean single channel.** The wire IS the control message\nfor reconfigure and delete; the utility-box only emits a\nvisible value on the create path (the new id, on the\nwire's normal data ring).\n\n### C3. The pointer pushed through RING_RECONFIGURE\n\nA single `box_t *`. The struct it points at is the fully-\nbuilt replacement — id, kind, lang/ref/fn (for call kinds),\nrouting, inputs[], n_connections + connections[], spec_idx\nresolved, input_slot_ids[] resolved by name-matching\nagainst OLD's ports, multi_spawn computed.\n\nFor a delete, the pointer is `NULL` and the tag is\nRING_RECONFIGURE — the mutator branch dispatches on NULL\nto do the delete-specific work (install NULL at the graph\nslot, no slot reuse).\n\nOR — alternative mechanic — add a separate\nRING_DELETE tag value (= 3) so the mutator doesn't have to\nbranch on NULL inside the RECONFIGURE path. The two-tag\nform lets the mutator's branch table stay flat. Marginal\npreference for the explicit tag; revisit once we see the\ncode shape.\n\n### C4. The slot-id mapping computed by the mutator\n\nWhen the mutator pops the RECONFIGURE cell and has NEW in\nhand, it builds NEW's `input_slot_ids[]` by port-name\nmatching against OLD:\n\n- for each port in NEW:\n  - if a port of the same name exists in OLD: NEW's\n    `input_slot_ids[i] = OLD->input_slot_ids[same-name-idx]`\n  - else: allocate a fresh slot via `slot_alloc(...)` with\n    NEW's port's sizing requirements.\n- for each port in OLD not in NEW: mark its slot id as\n  abandoned. Abandoned slots are read once for queued-value\n  count (for the discard JSONL event) and then released at\n  OLD-free time.\n\nThe slot ids are integers; the mapping result is an array\nof integers stored in NEW. The matching is deterministic\n(name strings, equal-comparison); no concurrency on the\nmapping itself because it happens entirely inside the\ngather-flag-held section.\n\n### C5. The JSONL events\n\nThree new event types, all emitted from the mutator\nsection while the gather flag is held (so the events'\norder matches the swap order in the transcript):\n\n- `box_reconfigure` — fired after the swap publishes.\n  Fields: box id, post-apply spec (re-serialised from\n  NEW), task id, worker idx, monotonic timestamp.\n- `reconfigure_discard` — fired once per abandoned port\n  with queued values. Fields: box id, port name, count of\n  dropped values, first value bytes (up to 256), task id,\n  worker idx, timestamp.\n- `box_delete` — fired when the operation is a delete.\n  Fields: box id, task id, worker idx, timestamp.\n\nPlus 322's addition to the push-event family:\n\n- existing `push` event grows a new `result` value:\n  `target-gone`. Fires when `push_one_connection` looks up\n  the target and gets NULL. In strict mode (322's env\n  knob), the producer's task also fails; otherwise the\n  push is dropped and the producer continues.\n\n---\n\n## Part D — the worker arrival flow, drawn carefully\n\nWalking through what one worker does on a single box, with\neach atomic op marked.\n\n```\nworker arrives at box id = X with a fresh task:\n\n[1] B = atomic_load_acquire(graph.boxes[X])\n    -- if B is NULL: box was deleted before we got here.\n       emit a push-target-gone event for the producer's\n       wire that woke us (if traceable), drop, exit.\n\n[2] expected = 0\n    won = atomic_cas_strong(&B->gather_flag, &expected, 1)\n           acquire on success, relaxed on failure\n    if not won:\n        YARQ(X)               -- push self back to pool tail\n        exit\n\n[3] atomic_fetch_add_acquire(&B->refcount, 1)\n\n[4] for each input port i of B:\n        peek B's input_slot_ids[i]'s ordering ring head\n            (per-slot spinlock, briefly)\n        cache the (tag, idx) tuple\n\n[5] branch on the cached peek results:\n\n    [5a] all heads are RING_NATIVE or RING_JSON values:\n         pop one cell per port (under the per-slot lock again)\n         release gather:\n             atomic_store_release(&B->gather_flag, 0)\n         invoke B's spec with the gathered values\n         push outputs through B's connections\n         prev = atomic_fetch_sub_release(&B->refcount, 1)\n         if prev == 1 and atomic_load_acquire(graph.boxes[X]) != B:\n             free B\n         exit\n\n    [5b] some head is RING_RECONFIGURE:\n         pop the box_t pointer (NEW) from that port's\n             data ring under the per-slot lock\n         resolve NEW->input_slot_ids by name-matching to B\n         note OLD's abandoned slots\n         atomic_store_release(&graph.boxes[X], NEW)\n         release gather:\n             atomic_store_release(&B->gather_flag, 0)\n         emit box_reconfigure event\n         emit reconfigure_discard events per abandoned slot\n         YARQ(X)               -- the new box may have queued values\n         prev = atomic_fetch_sub_release(&B->refcount, 1)\n         if prev == 1: free B (graph slot is NEW now, mismatch by definition)\n         exit\n\n    [5c] some port is empty (head not present):\n         release gather:\n             atomic_store_release(&B->gather_flag, 0)\n         prev = atomic_fetch_sub_release(&B->refcount, 1)\n         if prev == 1 and graph.boxes[X] != B: free B\n         exit\n```\n\nA couple of details worth noting on this trace:\n\n- Step [1] runs only once at the start of the fire. The\n  reference returned drives everything else. If the swap\n  happens between [1] and [3], we still operate on OLD —\n  that's intentional and safe (the +1 refcount in [3]\n  protects OLD's memory).\n- The release in step [5a]'s pop-then-release ordering is\n  important. We pop FIRST, then release the gather flag;\n  otherwise a concurrent worker could enter gather and\n  re-pop the same cells.\n- In step [5b], the mutator's release of the gather flag\n  on B is followed by a YARQ. The YARQ schedules a fresh\n  worker which will load `graph.boxes[X]` and see NEW. So\n  the \"release B's flag, then YARQ\" order is important —\n  if we YARQed first, the new worker might race with our\n  swap.\n\n### The delete variant\n\n```\n    [5b'] some head is RING_DELETE (or RING_RECONFIGURE with NULL payload):\n         pop the NULL/marker from that port's data ring\n         atomic_store_release(&graph.boxes[X], NULL)\n         release gather:\n             atomic_store_release(&B->gather_flag, 0)\n         emit box_delete event\n         (no YARQ — the box is gone; no follow-up work)\n         prev = atomic_fetch_sub_release(&B->refcount, 1)\n         if prev == 1: free B\n         (the abandoned slots all get released here too)\n         exit\n```\n\n---\n\n## Part E — the userflow\n\nHow a user actually expresses these operations in a map.\n\n### E1. Create a new box\n\nUser writes a map with at least these boxes (drawn\nabstractly, not as JSON):\n\n```\n[trigger] --output--> [maker] --new_id--> [connect-box] --true--> [done]\n                       ↑\n                    spec (literal)\n```\n\nWhere [maker] is a utility-box with one input port `spec`,\nliteral:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"kind\": \"call\",\n  \"lang\": \"lua\",\n  \"ref\": \"my_handler.lua\",\n  \"fn\": \"handle\"\n}\n```\n\nWhen the run starts, [trigger] fires, pushes a value, the\n[maker] gathers, parses the spec, sees no existing target\nat its output wire endpoint (because the output wire goes\nto a connect-box that doesn't yet exist as a target — or\nrather, [connect-box] IS the receiver of the new id, not\nthe target of the create). Hmm, this is the bit that's\ntangled.\n\nRe-think: the utility-box's output wire is the \"target\npointer\" ONLY for reconfigure/delete. For create, the\noutput wire IS just an output wire — the new box's id\ngets pushed as a value, downstream consumers receive it\nnormally.\n\nSo the disambiguation logic at the utility-box's fire is:\n\n```\nlook up the box at the wire endpoint\n  - if box exists: it's a reconfigure (or delete if spec says so)\n  - if no wire endpoint, or endpoint is the connect-box\n      (which is just a data consumer): it's a create\n```\n\nA cleaner disambiguator: distinguish by SPEC CONTENT, not\nby wire endpoint:\n\n- if spec has `\"action\": \"delete\"` → delete (target = wire endpoint)\n- if spec has `\"id\"` matching wire endpoint → reconfigure (target = wire endpoint)\n- if spec lacks `\"id\"` OR `\"id\"` doesn't match anything in the graph → create (no target; emit new id as value)\n\nThis makes the spec content authoritative and removes the\nambiguity. Wire endpoint is consulted ONLY to verify the\ntarget id matches.\n\n### E2. Reconfigure an existing box\n\n```\n[event_source] --output--> [reconfigure-spec-builder]\n                              --spec(json)--> [utility-box]\n                                                   --(wire)--> [target_X]\n```\n\nThe user wired [utility-box]'s output port to point at\n[target_X]'s any-input-port. (Convention: a dedicated port\nnamed `_reconfigure` on every box, OR any port works\nbecause the RING_RECONFIGURE tag is interpreted\ndistinctly. The simpler design uses any port — the tag\ndistinguishes.)\n\n[event_source] is whatever the user has — a timer, a UI\nevent, a periodic recheck. It fires, emits some trigger\nvalue. [reconfigure-spec-builder] is a normal call-box\n(any language) that builds the JSON spec — say, with a new\nfunction pointer based on the time of day — and emits the\nJSON string. [utility-box] receives it on its `spec` port,\nparses it, sees the spec carries `\"id\": \"target_X\"` that\nmatches its wire endpoint, builds NEW, pushes the\nRING_RECONFIGURE pointer into [target_X]'s input slot.\n\n[target_X]'s next worker enters gather, peeks, sees\nRING_RECONFIGURE, runs the mutator path, swaps the graph\nslot, releases, YARQs.\n\nThe user never wrote `runtime_apply_box_spec` in code. The\nmutation is expressed in the map.\n\n### E3. Delete a box\n\nSame shape as reconfigure; the spec carries\n`\"action\": \"delete\"` (per 322's explicit-marker design).\nThe utility-box sees the marker and pushes a RING_DELETE\n(or RING_RECONFIGURE with NULL payload) instead of a\npopulated NEW pointer.\n\n### E4. The convenience wrapper (322)\n\nFor users who want to delete from inside a normal call-box\n(Lua, C, Bash) without setting up a utility-box, 322\nexposes a sugar function:\n\n- Lua: `soramech.destroy_box(\"target_X\")`\n- C: `soramech_destroy_box(\"target_X\", err_buf, err_cap)`\n- Bash: `soramech_destroy_box \"target_X\"`\n\nThese all wrap an internal `runtime_apply_box_spec(id,\nspec_json)` that constructs the delete-action JSON and\nruns the same dispatch path. The mechanism is identical;\nonly the surface differs.\n\nThe same wrapper shape works for reconfigure-from-spec\nand create-from-spec, by passing different spec content.\nBut the box-and-wire path is the idiomatic one; the\nlanguage-bridge functions exist for cases where you want\nto imperatively mutate without rebuilding the map.\n\n### E5. The connections-append flow (321)\n\nToday: a user inside a Lua box calls\n`soramech.connect({from_box=\"A\", to_box=\"B\", to_input=\"x\"})`.\nThe bridge calls `runtime_connect`, which calls\n`box_add_connection` on box A. Box A's connections array\ngets atomically grown.\n\nAfter 321: the same Lua call surface, but internally\n`runtime_connect`:\n\n1. looks up box A,\n2. builds a NEW box record that is a copy of A's current\n   record with one extra entry appended to NEW->connections,\n3. calls `runtime_apply_box_spec(\"A\", spec_json_of_NEW)`\n   — which routes through the utility-box dispatch path:\n   builds the box_t struct, pushes the RING_RECONFIGURE\n   cell, returns.\n\nStep 3 actually skips the utility-box (there's no box to\npush from) and pushes directly into box A's input slot via\nan internal direct entry to the mutator queue. The user-\nfacing API is preserved; the mechanism collapses.\n\nThe user-visible difference: `soramech.connect` is now\nasync with respect to the actual application. The\nconnection appears once the next worker drains the\nRING_RECONFIGURE on box A. Today it's effectively\nsynchronous (the connections array is atomically grown\nright away). This is a behavioural change worth flagging.\n\n(Alternative: keep the synchronous semantics by adding a\nfast-path that, when called from within box A's own\ninvoke, mutates the connections in place under the gather\nflag. But that adds a special case for the common usage;\ndefer.)\n\n---\n\n## Part F — open design questions, in priority order\n\nThe shape is settled. These are the bits that want one\nmore pass of thinking before the implementation starts.\n\n### F1. Single channel vs side channel for the utility-box\n\nDecided above: lean single channel. The utility-box's\noutput wire is the operation pointer for reconfigure /\ndelete; the wire's bytes are NOT a normal value but a\nRING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell. The wire's bytes ARE a\nnormal value for create (the new id as a string).\n\nBut this means the utility-box's output wire has two\ndifferent semantic shapes depending on the operation.\nThat's fine programmatically (the tag distinguishes) but\nslightly confusing for users tracing maps.\n\nCleaner alternative: separate output ports on the\nutility-box:\n\n- `id_out` — only used on create, emits the new id\n- (implicit) the reconfigure/delete control message is\n  side-channeled to the target via the wire endpoint\n  attached to a dedicated `_control` port on the target\n\nThat keeps each wire's semantic shape consistent.\n\n**Question for you:** single-channel-with-mixed-semantics\nor separate-ports-by-operation?\n\n### F2. RING_DELETE vs RING_RECONFIGURE-with-NULL\n\nSame mechanism, different surface. The explicit RING_DELETE\ntag makes the mutator's branch table flatter; the implicit\n\"NULL payload means delete\" reuses one tag and slot.\n\n**Question for you:** which feels right?\n\n### F3. Disambiguation by spec content vs wire endpoint\n\nDecided above: spec content authoritative. The utility-box\ntrusts the spec's `action` field and `id` field; the wire\nendpoint is consulted only as a safety check (must match\nthe spec's id, when the spec carries one).\n\n**Question for you:** confirm or push back?\n\n### F4. The utility-box's name\n\nCurrently called BOX_CREATE_BOX (319f's name, narrow). The\nunified kind needs a new name. Candidates:\n\n- `utility-box` — the user's tentative pick. Vague but\n  accurate (it absorbs three operations).\n- `spec-applier` — more descriptive of what it does.\n- `box-control` — terse, matches the \"control message\"\n  framing.\n- `box-mutate` — matches the operation type.\n- `reconfigure-box` — narrow, but most user-facing\n  operations are reconfigures.\n\nInternal C enum name: BOX_UTILITY, BOX_SPEC_APPLIER,\nBOX_MUTATE, etc.\n\n**Question for you:** which?\n\n### F5. The strict-delete knob default\n\n322's design: silent-drop-by-default on push-to-deleted-\ntarget, with a JSONL event recording the loss. A knob\n(env var `SORAMECH_STRICT_DELETE=1` or a per-call flag)\nupgrades to hard error.\n\nThe user's general preference is \"errors over fallbacks.\"\nBut deletion is a deliberate user action; hard-erroring\nevery push to a deleted box can crash a long-running graph\non a single intentional delete.\n\n**Question for you:** silent-drop default with opt-in\nstrict, or strict default with opt-in silent?\n\n### F6. `runtime_connect`'s synchronous semantics\n\nToday connects are effectively immediate. After 321 they\nbecome async (drain when the next worker visits the\ntarget). Most users won't notice — connect is rarely\nfollowed by immediate use of the wire from the same task —\nbut it's a behavioural change.\n\n**Question for you:** accept the async semantics, or add\nthe same-task fast-path to preserve synchronous behaviour\nwhen `runtime_connect` is called from inside the producer\nbox's own invoke?\n\n### F7. The graph-slot array's per-cell atomicity\n\nRight now `g->boxes` is `_Atomic(box_t **)` — the array\npointer is atomic, the per-cell pointers are not. To\nsupport per-id atomic swap we either:\n\n- (a) make each cell `_Atomic(box_t *)`. Requires changing\n  the cell type and every reader/writer.\n- (b) use atomic builtins (`__atomic_store_n`,\n  `__atomic_load_n`) on the cells without changing the\n  type. Less invasive but bypasses C11's type system.\n\nOption (a) is correct. Some surface area touches.\n\n**Question for you:** confirm (a)?\n\n### F8. Slot reclamation on abandoned ports\n\nA port abandoned during reconfigure has queued values that\nwill never be drained. We emit a `reconfigure_discard`\nevent with the count and a sample. The slot itself —\nshould we free it (return its cells to the allocator),\nor leak it for the run's duration?\n\nToday the slot store has no `slot_free` (per 319f's\ncomment \"no slot_free in this slice — 315 territory\").\nReconfigure-abandoned slots inherit that. The bytes leak\nuntil run end.\n\n**Question for you:** accept the leak for now, or treat\nslot_free as a prerequisite of 320?\n\n---\n\n## Part G — implementation order\n\nA draft sequence. Each step is independently committable\nand testable. Numbers are not days; they're roughly\n\"this step before that step.\"\n\n1. **Make the graph slot per-cell atomic.** Change\n   `g->boxes`'s cell type. Update every reader and writer.\n   No behaviour change yet.\n2. **Add `gather_flag` and `refcount` fields to `box_t`.**\n   Initialise to 0 in both the loader and the runtime\n   creation path. No behaviour change yet.\n3. **Wrap `dispatch_action` with the gather CAS + refcount\n   bump.** YARQ on loss. Existing tests must still pass\n   because the lock is uncontended in the single-spawn\n   case.\n4. **Add a refcount-driven free hook in `dispatch_action`.**\n   On task end, decrement; if last and graph slot doesn't\n   match, free the box record. (Today it would never\n   trigger because nothing mutates the slot yet.)\n5. **Add the RING_RECONFIGURE tag to the slot store's\n   ordering ring.** Push helper, pop helper, peek helper.\n   No callers yet.\n6. **Implement the mutator path inside `dispatch_action`.**\n   The branch in step [5b] of Part D. Add the JSONL events.\n7. **Add the utility-box kind.** Reuse the existing\n   create_box machinery for the create path. Add the\n   reconfigure/delete spec parsing and the RING_RECONFIGURE\n   push.\n8. **Migrate `runtime_connect` to the new path (321).**\n   Drop the connections-array copy-and-publish. Drop the\n   `_Atomic` qualifiers.\n9. **Add `runtime_destroy_box` and the language bridges (322).**\n10. **Add the strict-delete knob.**\n11. **Write fixtures** for each path (the seven in 320, the\n    two in 322).\n12. **Update `docs/datapaths.md` (or equivalent)** with the\n    new ASCII diagrams of dataflow.\n\n---\n\n## Part H — summary table of atomic ops\n\n| field | type | writer | reader | ordering |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| graph.boxes[id] | `_Atomic(box_t *)` | mutator (swap) | worker (lookup) | release / acquire |\n| box.gather_flag | `_Atomic int` | worker (CAS 0↔1) | worker (same CAS) | acquire on win / release on free |\n| box.refcount | `_Atomic int` | worker + mutator (fetch_add/sub) | freer (post-sub) | acquire on bump / release on drop |\n| slot ordering-ring head | per-slot lock | producer (push) | consumer (peek/pop) | per-slot spinlock |\n| ring data cell (RING_RECONFIGURE) | per-slot lock | producer (push of `box_t *` bytes) | consumer (pop, holds gather flag) | per-slot spinlock + gather flag |\n| box.n_connections / .connections | plain (after 321) | mutator (writes NEW pre-swap) | consumer (reads via NEW after swap) | happens-before via slot release/acquire |\n\nAll of these except the per-slot spinlocks are lock-free.\nThe per-slot spinlocks already exist (issue 302 territory)\nand aren't changed.\n\n---\n\n## Part I — what changes in which file\n\n- `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}` — add RING_RECONFIGURE (and\n  maybe RING_DELETE) to the tag enum; the dual-ring push /\n  pop / peek helpers grow new variants for the control\n  tag.\n- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — make the `boxes` array's\n  cell type `_Atomic(box_t *)`; add `gather_flag` and\n  `refcount` fields to `box_t`; drop the `_Atomic`\n  qualifiers on `n_connections` and `connections` (after\n  321); add a `box_t *` free helper that's the right\n  shape for the refcount-driven path.\n- `src/012-dispatch.{c,h}` — wrap the action with the\n  gather CAS and refcount bump; add the mutator branch;\n  add the BOX_UTILITY case (replacing or subsuming\n  BOX_CREATE_BOX and BOX_CONNECT).\n- `src/013-jsonl-events.{c,h}` /\n  `src/014-event-queue.{c,h}` — add the three new event\n  types (`box_reconfigure`, `reconfigure_discard`,\n  `box_delete`) and the new `target-gone` push result.\n- `src/018-runtime-builtins.{c,h}` — re-implement\n  `runtime_connect` over the new path; add\n  `runtime_apply_box_spec` and `runtime_destroy_box` as\n  internal entry points; the C glue for the language\n  bridges.\n- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c`\n  — one new bridge function each: `destroy_box`.\n\n---\n\n## End-state, in one sentence\n\nA box's identity is owned by a per-id atomic pointer in\nthe graph slot; mutation happens by building a fully-\ntyped replacement off the hot path, pushing its pointer\nthrough an input wire tagged as control, and letting the\ntarget's next worker do the swap under a per-box gather\nlock with a refcount to govern the old record's free. The\nsame mechanism handles create / reconfigure / delete, and\nthe same mechanism absorbs the connections-append\noperation that used to be its own special case.\n",
```

```json
    "filePath":"/tmp/soramech-320-321-322-plan.md",
    "content":"# 320 / 321 / 322 — planning notes\n\nA planning document, not a spec. Stored at /tmp/ during the\ndesign conversation; rewrite into /docs/ once the open\nquestions at the bottom are resolved.\n\nThe three issues form one feature with one shared mechanism.\nThe piece that does the work is 320 (the box-swap path\nthrough a unified utility-box kind). 321 retires the\nexisting connections-append dance by routing it through the\nsame swap. 322 is the user-facing shape and the policy\nchoices around delete.\n\nThis document tries to make every atomic interaction and\nevery byte that crosses a thread boundary explicit.\n\n---\n\n## Part A — the shape, in cleartext\n\nA running graph today is a directed bag of boxes whose\nidentity is frozen at load. The graph's box index is an\narray of pointers, one entry per box id. The box record\nbehind each pointer holds the function, the input port\ndeclarations, the routing kind, and the outgoing\nconnections.\n\nThe change: that per-id pointer becomes the only handle\nthat's allowed to mutate, and the mutation is always the\nsame shape — install a fully-built replacement box at the\nslot, retire the old. Three user-visible operations\ncollapse onto that one mechanism:\n\n- **create**: no existing box at the id; the slot is fresh.\n  The replacement IS the new box.\n- **reconfigure**: an existing box; the replacement carries\n  the same id, possibly a different function, possibly\n  different ports, possibly different connections.\n- **delete**: an existing box; the replacement is NULL.\n\nAll three reach the runtime as JSON specs on the input wire\nof a single box kind — the utility-box. The utility-box\nreads its `spec` input port, parses, decides which of the\nthree operations the spec describes, and acts.\n\nThe wire endpoint of the utility-box's output is the\ntarget. There is no `target_id` input. \"This utility-box\noperates on box X\" means \"this utility-box's output wire\nends at one of X's input ports.\"\n\n---\n\n## Part B — atomic interactions, enumerated\n\nEvery shared piece of state across threads, what touches it,\nwhat ordering guarantees we depend on, and what the\ninvariant is.\n\n### B1. The graph slot pointer\n\n**Storage.** `graph_t::boxes` is `_Atomic(box_t **)`, an\narray of box pointers. Each entry `boxes[i]` is the per-id\nslot. Today the array itself can grow (copy-and-publish\nunder a graph mutex); the per-id pointer is set once at\nload and never changes.\n\n**Change.** The per-id pointer becomes mutable. A mutator\natomic-stores a replacement pointer at `boxes[i]`. Readers\natomic-load it.\n\n**The atomic op.**\n\n- writer: `atomic_store_explicit(&boxes[i], NEW, memory_order_release)`\n- reader: `atomic_load_explicit(&boxes[i], memory_order_acquire)`\n\nThe release/acquire pair ensures any reader who observes\nNEW also observes everything the mutator wrote into NEW's\nfields before publishing.\n\nNULL is a valid value (it means \"deleted\"). Readers must\ntolerate NULL gracefully.\n\n**Invariant.** \"The current box for id i is whatever\n`boxes[i]` points at right now.\" Workers who looked up\nboxes[i] before the swap and got OLD finish their work on\nOLD; they never re-resolve mid-task.\n\n**Open mechanical detail.** The per-id storage is today a\nsingle pointer per slot. We need to either keep it that way\n(an array of `_Atomic(box_t *)`) or change the surrounding\ninfrastructure to allow per-id atomic stores. The current\ncopy-and-publish growth path stores `box_t **` non-atomically\n(it's just the array, not the cells). The cells need\natomic-store semantics. Cleanest: make the array's cell\ntype `_Atomic(box_t *)`.\n\n### B2. The gather flag\n\n**Storage.** A new field on `box_t`, `_Atomic int gather_flag`.\nOne byte of meaning, two states:\n\n- 0 — free; any worker may try to enter.\n- 1 — held; exactly one worker is currently in the gather\n  section of this box.\n\n**Why per-box, not per-port.** A box's gather is a logical\noperation over all input ports of that box — peeking each\nordering ring head, deciding whether to pop or yield-and-\nrequeue, popping consistently. Multiple workers could\nin principle be at this same box, but only one can do the\npeek-then-pop without racing with another's pop. The\nper-box flag is the smallest serialisation that protects\nthat section. Different boxes don't contend.\n\n**The atomic op.**\n\n- enter: `atomic_compare_exchange_strong(&b->gather_flag, &expected=0, 1)`\n  with memory_order_acquire on success, memory_order_relaxed\n  on failure.\n- release: `atomic_store_explicit(&b->gather_flag, 0, memory_order_release)`.\n\n**Behaviour on contention.** Lose → yield-and-requeue\n(push the current task back to the pool's tail with\npriority 0, return without doing any work for this fire).\nNo spinning, no waiting. The runtime is busy elsewhere; come\nback later.\n\n**Invariant.** \"While a worker is between peek and pop on\nthis box's input ports, no other worker is doing the same\non this box.\"\n\n### B3. The reference count\n\n**Storage.** A new field on `box_t`, `_Atomic int refcount`.\n\n**Who increments.**\n\n- **Workers** that arrive at the box and have just won the\n  gather-flag CAS. They bump refcount to mark \"I am\n  currently executing on this box record.\" The bump happens\n  AFTER the CAS win so a freer that observes refcount == 0\n  also knows no worker is in mid-fire.\n- **Mutators** (the worker that is about to push a swap)\n  bump refcount before publishing the new pointer. This is\n  the \"+1 hold\" — it keeps the box alive long enough for\n  the mutator to finish even if every other worker has\n  already left.\n\n**Who decrements.**\n\n- Workers at the end of their fire, after pushing outputs.\n- The mutator after publishing the new pointer.\n\n**The atomic op.**\n\n- bump: `atomic_fetch_add_explicit(&b->refcount, 1, memory_order_acquire)`\n- drop: `atomic_fetch_sub_explicit(&b->refcount, 1, memory_order_release)`\n  returns previous value; if previous was 1, the dropper is\n  the last reference.\n\n**The free condition.** The last decrementer (previous\nvalue 1, so post-decrement is 0) checks the graph slot for\nthis box id. If `boxes[id]` no longer points at THIS box,\nthe dropper owns the free. If `boxes[id]` still points at\nTHIS box, do nothing — a future mutator will see this\nrefcount when it lands.\n\n**Why the mutator's +1 matters.** Sequence without it:\nmutator computes NEW, publishes at boxes[id], decrements\nrefcount, frees OLD. But another worker that was mid-fire\non OLD between the mutator's publish and the mutator's\ndecrement might still touch OLD's fields. The +1 hold\nprevents that — the mutator's hold keeps OLD alive across\nthe publish; the last drop is whichever worker leaves\nlast, and that drop happens-after every other touch.\n\n**Invariant.** \"A box is free-able exactly when (a) the\ngraph slot doesn't point at it AND (b) refcount has\ndropped to zero.\" Whoever observes both conditions owns\nthe free.\n\n### B4. The ordering ring's RING_RECONFIGURE tag\n\n**Storage.** Today the ordering ring on a dual-ring slot\nstores (which_ring, idx) tuples where which_ring is\nSLOT_RING_NATIVE (0) or SLOT_RING_JSON (1). The data lives\nin two separate native/JSON rings; the ordering ring\npreserves arrival order across both.\n\n**Change.** Add a third tag value, RING_RECONFIGURE (= 2).\nA cell tagged RING_RECONFIGURE carries the index of a\ncontrol-message slot whose payload is a `box_t *` (the\nfully-built replacement). For uniformity we either:\n\n- (a) push the box pointer through the existing native or\n  JSON ring as 8 bytes (the pointer value) and use the new\n  tag to mark it as \"interpret these bytes as a pointer,\n  not as a value\", OR\n- (b) add a third internal ring on the slot — a small\n  ring of `box_t *` pointers — and the tag means \"look at\n  ring C, not A or B\".\n\nOption (a) is cheaper (no new ring) and reuses the\nallocator. The cell capacity needs to be at least 8 bytes\nfor a 64-bit pointer; most slots already are. Option (b)\nis cleaner-typed but adds a third ring everywhere.\n\nLean (a) for the first slice. The control cell's payload\nis exactly `sizeof(box_t *)` bytes — the pointer to NEW.\n\n**The atomic op.** Pushing RING_RECONFIGURE is the same\nper-slot push under the existing per-slot spinlock; the\nordering tuple's atomicity comes from being written under\nthe same lock as the data ring's index advance.\n\n**Why peek-then-pop is safe.** The gather-flag held by a\nworker doesn't serialise pushes (pushes are per-slot, not\nper-box). But it serialises peeks and pops from the SAME\nworker, so if a worker peeks port P and observes\nRING_RECONFIGURE at the head, the subsequent pop on port P\nreturns that same RING_RECONFIGURE cell — because the per-\nslot ring is FIFO and the gather-lock-holding worker is\nthe only one popping this box's input slots right now.\nPushes after the peek append at the tail; they don't\ndisplace the head.\n\n**Invariant.** \"If a worker holds box B's gather flag and\npeeks port P's ordering ring head as X, the next pop on\nport P returns X.\" This is what makes the design lock-free\non the producer side and lock-protected only on the\nconsumer's gather section.\n\n### B5. The connections array (321's territory)\n\n**Today.** `box_t::n_connections` and `box_t::connections`\nare atomic. `box_add_connection` allocates a new array,\ncopies + appends, atomic-stores the array pointer first\n(release), then the count (release). Readers in dispatch\nload with acquire and see either the pre-append snapshot\nor the post-append snapshot, never a torn mix. The old\narray is parked on a stale list, freed at graph_destroy.\n\n**After 321.** The connections array is mutated only as\npart of building a NEW box for the swap. The NEW box's\nconnections are written before the swap publishes NEW at\nthe graph slot, so by the time any reader walks NEW's\nconnections array the writes have already happened-before\nthe slot's atomic-store. The atomic qualifiers come off;\nplain pointer + plain int is enough.\n\nThe stale list keeps existing function — old box records\nget parked when a swap happens, freed at refcount-zero +\ngraph-slot mismatch. The per-connection-array staleness\ncollapses into per-box staleness.\n\n### B6. Yield-and-requeue (YARQ)\n\n**Not an atomic primitive; a control-flow primitive.** A\nworker that loses the gather CAS, or a mutator that has\njust published the swap, calls back into\n`dispatch_spawn` with the same box id and priority 0.\nThe task lands at the tail of the pool's queue.\n\nThe mutator's YARQ in step 7 is load-bearing for a subtle\nreason. Queued values on the box's input slots survive the\nswap (the slots are owned per-port, identified by slot id\nwhich the new box record may or may not have inherited\ndepending on port-name match). Without the YARQ, those\nqueued values just sit there until the next producer\npushes — which might be never. The YARQ schedules a fire\non the new box that drains them.\n\n---\n\n## Part C — the values on the wires\n\n### C1. What the utility-box receives on its `spec` input\n\nA single string-shaped value, JSON object syntax. Same\nshape as the on-disk `boxes/<id>.json` file. Three rough\nflavours:\n\n**Create:**\n\n```json\n{\n  \"kind\": \"call\",\n  \"lang\": \"lua\",\n  \"ref\": \"my_fn.lua\",\n  \"fn\": \"do_thing\",\n  \"inputs\": [{\"name\": \"x\", \"type\": \"string\"}],\n  \"routing\": {\"kind\": \"plain\"}\n}\n```\n\nNo id; the runtime auto-generates. Alternatively an\nexplicit `\"id\": \"...\"` can be supplied. No connections —\nthe utility-box's output wire delivers the new id to a\ndownstream consumer (typically a connect box), and that\nconsumer wires it up.\n\n**Reconfigure (implicit, the inferred form):**\n\n```json\n{\n  \"kind\": \"call\",\n  \"lang\": \"lua\",\n  \"ref\": \"my_fn.lua\",\n  \"fn\": \"do_thing_v2\",\n  \"inputs\": [{\"name\": \"x\", \"type\": \"string\"}]\n}\n```\n\nNo `action` field — the utility-box infers from the wire\nendpoint. The wire ends at box X's `whatever` port → the\noperation targets X.\n\n**Delete (explicit, per 322):**\n\n```json\n{ \"action\": \"delete\" }\n```\n\nOr with a redundant id check:\n\n```json\n{ \"action\": \"delete\", \"id\": \"X\" }\n```\n\nIf `id` is present, the runtime checks it matches the wire\nendpoint and errors out on mismatch — a safety check\nagainst accidentally targeting the wrong box.\n\n### C2. What the utility-box emits on its output\n\nDepends on which operation it dispatched:\n\n- **create**: emits the new box's id as a string. Length =\n  strlen(id). Same shape as today's BOX_CREATE_BOX output.\n  Downstream consumers receive an id and can build connect\n  specs that reference it.\n- **reconfigure**: emits the literal string \"true\" (or\n  similar success marker) on the side-effect output. The\n  primary effect is the swap; the output is just for chain-\n  ability.\n- **delete**: emits \"true\" on the side-effect output.\n\nThe wire from the utility-box's output to the target's\ninput port is the OPERATION POINTER — the bytes on it are\nnot what triggers the operation, the act of pushing them\nthrough this particular wire is. For reconfigure and\ndelete, the bytes are arbitrary (we emit \"true\" for\nchainability; the target ignores them and processes the\nRING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell that was pushed alongside).\n\nWait — this is the bit that needs precision. Two possible\nshapes:\n\n- **Single channel:** the utility-box pushes the\n  RING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell through its output wire.\n  That tagged cell carries the `box_t *` to NEW. The\n  target observes a RING_RECONFIGURE at the port the wire\n  ends at and goes into the mutator branch.\n- **Side channel:** the utility-box looks up the target\n  by walking its own output wire, builds NEW, pushes the\n  RING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell DIRECTLY to the target's\n  slot (not through its own output ring at all), and then\n  separately pushes \"true\" through its output for the\n  visible side effect.\n\nThe single-channel form is cleaner — one push, one wire,\none target — and matches the issue's stated mechanism\n(\"push to the target box's input slot\"). The side-channel\nform lets the utility-box's output also drive downstream\nchains for free; but that's a marginal convenience versus\nthe conceptual cost of \"the wire is a control message in\ndisguise.\"\n\n**Lean single channel.** The wire IS the control message\nfor reconfigure and delete; the utility-box only emits a\nvisible value on the create path (the new id, on the\nwire's normal data ring).\n\n### C3. The pointer pushed through RING_RECONFIGURE\n\nA single `box_t *`. The struct it points at is the fully-\nbuilt replacement — id, kind, lang/ref/fn (for call kinds),\nrouting, inputs[], n_connections + connections[], spec_idx\nresolved, input_slot_ids[] resolved by name-matching\nagainst OLD's ports, multi_spawn computed.\n\nFor a delete, the pointer is `NULL` and the tag is\nRING_RECONFIGURE — the mutator branch dispatches on NULL\nto do the delete-specific work (install NULL at the graph\nslot, no slot reuse).\n\nOR — alternative mechanic — add a separate\nRING_DELETE tag value (= 3) so the mutator doesn't have to\nbranch on NULL inside the RECONFIGURE path. The two-tag\nform lets the mutator's branch table stay flat. Marginal\npreference for the explicit tag; revisit once we see the\ncode shape.\n\n### C4. The slot-id mapping computed by the mutator\n\nWhen the mutator pops the RECONFIGURE cell and has NEW in\nhand, it builds NEW's `input_slot_ids[]` by port-name\nmatching against OLD:\n\n- for each port in NEW:\n  - if a port of the same name exists in OLD: NEW's\n    `input_slot_ids[i] = OLD->input_slot_ids[same-name-idx]`\n  - else: allocate a fresh slot via `slot_alloc(...)` with\n    NEW's port's sizing requirements.\n- for each port in OLD not in NEW: mark its slot id as\n  abandoned. Abandoned slots are read once for queued-value\n  count (for the discard JSONL event) and then released at\n  OLD-free time.\n\nThe slot ids are integers; the mapping result is an array\nof integers stored in NEW. The matching is deterministic\n(name strings, equal-comparison); no concurrency on the\nmapping itself because it happens entirely inside the\ngather-flag-held section.\n\n### C5. The JSONL events\n\nThree new event types, all emitted from the mutator\nsection while the gather flag is held (so the events'\norder matches the swap order in the transcript):\n\n- `box_reconfigure` — fired after the swap publishes.\n  Fields: box id, post-apply spec (re-serialised from\n  NEW), task id, worker idx, monotonic timestamp.\n- `reconfigure_discard` — fired once per abandoned port\n  with queued values. Fields: box id, port name, count of\n  dropped values, first value bytes (up to 256), task id,\n  worker idx, timestamp.\n- `box_delete` — fired when the operation is a delete.\n  Fields: box id, task id, worker idx, timestamp.\n\nPlus 322's addition to the push-event family:\n\n- existing `push` event grows a new `result` value:\n  `target-gone`. Fires when `push_one_connection` looks up\n  the target and gets NULL. In strict mode (322's env\n  knob), the producer's task also fails; otherwise the\n  push is dropped and the producer continues.\n\n---\n\n## Part D — the worker arrival flow, drawn carefully\n\nWalking through what one worker does on a single box, with\neach atomic op marked.\n\n```\nworker arrives at box id = X with a fresh task:\n\n[1] B = atomic_load_acquire(graph.boxes[X])\n    -- if B is NULL: box was deleted before we got here.\n       emit a push-target-gone event for the producer's\n       wire that woke us (if traceable), drop, exit.\n\n[2] expected = 0\n    won = atomic_cas_strong(&B->gather_flag, &expected, 1)\n           acquire on success, relaxed on failure\n    if not won:\n        YARQ(X)               -- push self back to pool tail\n        exit\n\n[3] atomic_fetch_add_acquire(&B->refcount, 1)\n\n[4] for each input port i of B:\n        peek B's input_slot_ids[i]'s ordering ring head\n            (per-slot spinlock, briefly)\n        cache the (tag, idx) tuple\n\n[5] branch on the cached peek results:\n\n    [5a] all heads are RING_NATIVE or RING_JSON values:\n         pop one cell per port (under the per-slot lock again)\n         release gather:\n             atomic_store_release(&B->gather_flag, 0)\n         invoke B's spec with the gathered values\n         push outputs through B's connections\n         prev = atomic_fetch_sub_release(&B->refcount, 1)\n         if prev == 1 and atomic_load_acquire(graph.boxes[X]) != B:\n             free B\n         exit\n\n    [5b] some head is RING_RECONFIGURE:\n         pop the box_t pointer (NEW) from that port's\n             data ring under the per-slot lock\n         resolve NEW->input_slot_ids by name-matching to B\n         note OLD's abandoned slots\n         atomic_store_release(&graph.boxes[X], NEW)\n         release gather:\n             atomic_store_release(&B->gather_flag, 0)\n         emit box_reconfigure event\n         emit reconfigure_discard events per abandoned slot\n         YARQ(X)               -- the new box may have queued values\n         prev = atomic_fetch_sub_release(&B->refcount, 1)\n         if prev == 1: free B (graph slot is NEW now, mismatch by definition)\n         exit\n\n    [5c] some port is empty (head not present):\n         release gather:\n             atomic_store_release(&B->gather_flag, 0)\n         prev = atomic_fetch_sub_release(&B->refcount, 1)\n         if prev == 1 and graph.boxes[X] != B: free B\n         exit\n```\n\nA couple of details worth noting on this trace:\n\n- Step [1] runs only once at the start of the fire. The\n  reference returned drives everything else. If the swap\n  happens between [1] and [3], we still operate on OLD —\n  that's intentional and safe (the +1 refcount in [3]\n  protects OLD's memory).\n- The release in step [5a]'s pop-then-release ordering is\n  important. We pop FIRST, then release the gather flag;\n  otherwise a concurrent worker could enter gather and\n  re-pop the same cells.\n- In step [5b], the mutator's release of the gather flag\n  on B is followed by a YARQ. The YARQ schedules a fresh\n  worker which will load `graph.boxes[X]` and see NEW. So\n  the \"release B's flag, then YARQ\" order is important —\n  if we YARQed first, the new worker might race with our\n  swap.\n\n### The delete variant\n\n```\n    [5b'] some head is RING_DELETE (or RING_RECONFIGURE with NULL payload):\n         pop the NULL/marker from that port's data ring\n         atomic_store_release(&graph.boxes[X], NULL)\n         release gather:\n             atomic_store_release(&B->gather_flag, 0)\n         emit box_delete event\n         (no YARQ — the box is gone; no follow-up work)\n         prev = atomic_fetch_sub_release(&B->refcount, 1)\n         if prev == 1: free B\n         (the abandoned slots all get released here too)\n         exit\n```\n\n---\n\n## Part E — the userflow\n\nHow a user actually expresses these operations in a map.\n\n### E1. Create a new box\n\nUser writes a map with at least these boxes (drawn\nabstractly, not as JSON):\n\n```\n[trigger] --output--> [maker] --new_id--> [connect-box] --true--> [done]\n                       ↑\n                    spec (literal)\n```\n\nWhere [maker] is a utility-box with one input port `spec`,\nliteral:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"kind\": \"call\",\n  \"lang\": \"lua\",\n  \"ref\": \"my_handler.lua\",\n  \"fn\": \"handle\"\n}\n```\n\nWhen the run starts, [trigger] fires, pushes a value, the\n[maker] gathers, parses the spec, sees no existing target\nat its output wire endpoint (because the output wire goes\nto a connect-box that doesn't yet exist as a target — or\nrather, [connect-box] IS the receiver of the new id, not\nthe target of the create). Hmm, this is the bit that's\ntangled.\n\nRe-think: the utility-box's output wire is the \"target\npointer\" ONLY for reconfigure/delete. For create, the\noutput wire IS just an output wire — the new box's id\ngets pushed as a value, downstream consumers receive it\nnormally.\n\nSo the disambiguation logic at the utility-box's fire is:\n\n```\nlook up the box at the wire endpoint\n  - if box exists: it's a reconfigure (or delete if spec says so)\n  - if no wire endpoint, or endpoint is the connect-box\n      (which is just a data consumer): it's a create\n```\n\nA cleaner disambiguator: distinguish by SPEC CONTENT, not\nby wire endpoint:\n\n- if spec has `\"action\": \"delete\"` → delete (target = wire endpoint)\n- if spec has `\"id\"` matching wire endpoint → reconfigure (target = wire endpoint)\n- if spec lacks `\"id\"` OR `\"id\"` doesn't match anything in the graph → create (no target; emit new id as value)\n\nThis makes the spec content authoritative and removes the\nambiguity. Wire endpoint is consulted ONLY to verify the\ntarget id matches.\n\n### E2. Reconfigure an existing box\n\n```\n[event_source] --output--> [reconfigure-spec-builder]\n                              --spec(json)--> [utility-box]\n                                                   --(wire)--> [target_X]\n```\n\nThe user wired [utility-box]'s output port to point at\n[target_X]'s any-input-port. (Convention: a dedicated port\nnamed `_reconfigure` on every box, OR any port works\nbecause the RING_RECONFIGURE tag is interpreted\ndistinctly. The simpler design uses any port — the tag\ndistinguishes.)\n\n[event_source] is whatever the user has — a timer, a UI\nevent, a periodic recheck. It fires, emits some trigger\nvalue. [reconfigure-spec-builder] is a normal call-box\n(any language) that builds the JSON spec — say, with a new\nfunction pointer based on the time of day — and emits the\nJSON string. [utility-box] receives it on its `spec` port,\nparses it, sees the spec carries `\"id\": \"target_X\"` that\nmatches its wire endpoint, builds NEW, pushes the\nRING_RECONFIGURE pointer into [target_X]'s input slot.\n\n[target_X]'s next worker enters gather, peeks, sees\nRING_RECONFIGURE, runs the mutator path, swaps the graph\nslot, releases, YARQs.\n\nThe user never wrote `runtime_apply_box_spec` in code. The\nmutation is expressed in the map.\n\n### E3. Delete a box\n\nSame shape as reconfigure; the spec carries\n`\"action\": \"delete\"` (per 322's explicit-marker design).\nThe utility-box sees the marker and pushes a RING_DELETE\n(or RING_RECONFIGURE with NULL payload) instead of a\npopulated NEW pointer.\n\n### E4. The convenience wrapper (322)\n\nFor users who want to delete from inside a normal call-box\n(Lua, C, Bash) without setting up a utility-box, 322\nexposes a sugar function:\n\n- Lua: `soramech.destroy_box(\"target_X\")`\n- C: `soramech_destroy_box(\"target_X\", err_buf, err_cap)`\n- Bash: `soramech_destroy_box \"target_X\"`\n\nThese all wrap an internal `runtime_apply_box_spec(id,\nspec_json)` that constructs the delete-action JSON and\nruns the same dispatch path. The mechanism is identical;\nonly the surface differs.\n\nThe same wrapper shape works for reconfigure-from-spec\nand create-from-spec, by passing different spec content.\nBut the box-and-wire path is the idiomatic one; the\nlanguage-bridge functions exist for cases where you want\nto imperatively mutate without rebuilding the map.\n\n### E5. The connections-append flow (321)\n\nToday: a user inside a Lua box calls\n`soramech.connect({from_box=\"A\", to_box=\"B\", to_input=\"x\"})`.\nThe bridge calls `runtime_connect`, which calls\n`box_add_connection` on box A. Box A's connections array\ngets atomically grown.\n\nAfter 321: the same Lua call surface, but internally\n`runtime_connect`:\n\n1. looks up box A,\n2. builds a NEW box record that is a copy of A's current\n   record with one extra entry appended to NEW->connections,\n3. calls `runtime_apply_box_spec(\"A\", spec_json_of_NEW)`\n   — which routes through the utility-box dispatch path:\n   builds the box_t struct, pushes the RING_RECONFIGURE\n   cell, returns.\n\nStep 3 actually skips the utility-box (there's no box to\npush from) and pushes directly into box A's input slot via\nan internal direct entry to the mutator queue. The user-\nfacing API is preserved; the mechanism collapses.\n\nThe user-visible difference: `soramech.connect` is now\nasync with respect to the actual application. The\nconnection appears once the next worker drains the\nRING_RECONFIGURE on box A. Today it's effectively\nsynchronous (the connections array is atomically grown\nright away). This is a behavioural change worth flagging.\n\n(Alternative: keep the synchronous semantics by adding a\nfast-path that, when called from within box A's own\ninvoke, mutates the connections in place under the gather\nflag. But that adds a special case for the common usage;\ndefer.)\n\n---\n\n## Part F — open design questions, in priority order\n\nThe shape is settled. These are the bits that want one\nmore pass of thinking before the implementation starts.\n\n### F1. Single channel vs side channel for the utility-box\n\nDecided above: lean single channel. The utility-box's\noutput wire is the operation pointer for reconfigure /\ndelete; the wire's bytes are NOT a normal value but a\nRING_RECONFIGURE-tagged cell. The wire's bytes ARE a\nnormal value for create (the new id as a string).\n\nBut this means the utility-box's output wire has two\ndifferent semantic shapes depending on the operation.\nThat's fine programmatically (the tag distinguishes) but\nslightly confusing for users tracing maps.\n\nCleaner alternative: separate output ports on the\nutility-box:\n\n- `id_out` — only used on create, emits the new id\n- (implicit) the reconfigure/delete control message is\n  side-channeled to the target via the wire endpoint\n  attached to a dedicated `_control` port on the target\n\nThat keeps each wire's semantic shape consistent.\n\n**Question for you:** single-channel-with-mixed-semantics\nor separate-ports-by-operation?\n\n### F2. RING_DELETE vs RING_RECONFIGURE-with-NULL\n\nSame mechanism, different surface. The explicit RING_DELETE\ntag makes the mutator's branch table flatter; the implicit\n\"NULL payload means delete\" reuses one tag and slot.\n\n**Question for you:** which feels right?\n\n### F3. Disambiguation by spec content vs wire endpoint\n\nDecided above: spec content authoritative. The utility-box\ntrusts the spec's `action` field and `id` field; the wire\nendpoint is consulted only as a safety check (must match\nthe spec's id, when the spec carries one).\n\n**Question for you:** confirm or push back?\n\n### F4. The utility-box's name\n\nCurrently called BOX_CREATE_BOX (319f's name, narrow). The\nunified kind needs a new name. Candidates:\n\n- `utility-box` — the user's tentative pick. Vague but\n  accurate (it absorbs three operations).\n- `spec-applier` — more descriptive of what it does.\n- `box-control` — terse, matches the \"control message\"\n  framing.\n- `box-mutate` — matches the operation type.\n- `reconfigure-box` — narrow, but most user-facing\n  operations are reconfigures.\n\nInternal C enum name: BOX_UTILITY, BOX_SPEC_APPLIER,\nBOX_MUTATE, etc.\n\n**Question for you:** which?\n\n### F5. The strict-delete knob default\n\n322's design: silent-drop-by-default on push-to-deleted-\ntarget, with a JSONL event recording the loss. A knob\n(env var `SORAMECH_STRICT_DELETE=1` or a per-call flag)\nupgrades to hard error.\n\nThe user's general preference is \"errors over fallbacks.\"\nBut deletion is a deliberate user action; hard-erroring\nevery push to a deleted box can crash a long-running graph\non a single intentional delete.\n\n**Question for you:** silent-drop default with opt-in\nstrict, or strict default with opt-in silent?\n\n### F6. `runtime_connect`'s synchronous semantics\n\nToday connects are effectively immediate. After 321 they\nbecome async (drain when the next worker visits the\ntarget). Most users won't notice — connect is rarely\nfollowed by immediate use of the wire from the same task —\nbut it's a behavioural change.\n\n**Question for you:** accept the async semantics, or add\nthe same-task fast-path to preserve synchronous behaviour\nwhen `runtime_connect` is called from inside the producer\nbox's own invoke?\n\n### F7. The graph-slot array's per-cell atomicity\n\nRight now `g->boxes` is `_Atomic(box_t **)` — the array\npointer is atomic, the per-cell pointers are not. To\nsupport per-id atomic swap we either:\n\n- (a) make each cell `_Atomic(box_t *)`. Requires changing\n  the cell type and every reader/writer.\n- (b) use atomic builtins (`__atomic_store_n`,\n  `__atomic_load_n`) on the cells without changing the\n  type. Less invasive but bypasses C11's type system.\n\nOption (a) is correct. Some surface area touches.\n\n**Question for you:** confirm (a)?\n\n### F8. Slot reclamation on abandoned ports\n\nA port abandoned during reconfigure has queued values that\nwill never be drained. We emit a `reconfigure_discard`\nevent with the count and a sample. The slot itself —\nshould we free it (return its cells to the allocator),\nor leak it for the run's duration?\n\nToday the slot store has no `slot_free` (per 319f's\ncomment \"no slot_free in this slice — 315 territory\").\nReconfigure-abandoned slots inherit that. The bytes leak\nuntil run end.\n\n**Question for you:** accept the leak for now, or treat\nslot_free as a prerequisite of 320?\n\n---\n\n## Part G — implementation order\n\nA draft sequence. Each step is independently committable\nand testable. Numbers are not days; they're roughly\n\"this step before that step.\"\n\n1. **Make the graph slot per-cell atomic.** Change\n   `g->boxes`'s cell type. Update every reader and writer.\n   No behaviour change yet.\n2. **Add `gather_flag` and `refcount` fields to `box_t`.**\n   Initialise to 0 in both the loader and the runtime\n   creation path. No behaviour change yet.\n3. **Wrap `dispatch_action` with the gather CAS + refcount\n   bump.** YARQ on loss. Existing tests must still pass\n   because the lock is uncontended in the single-spawn\n   case.\n4. **Add a refcount-driven free hook in `dispatch_action`.**\n   On task end, decrement; if last and graph slot doesn't\n   match, free the box record. (Today it would never\n   trigger because nothing mutates the slot yet.)\n5. **Add the RING_RECONFIGURE tag to the slot store's\n   ordering ring.** Push helper, pop helper, peek helper.\n   No callers yet.\n6. **Implement the mutator path inside `dispatch_action`.**\n   The branch in step [5b] of Part D. Add the JSONL events.\n7. **Add the utility-box kind.** Reuse the existing\n   create_box machinery for the create path. Add the\n   reconfigure/delete spec parsing and the RING_RECONFIGURE\n   push.\n8. **Migrate `runtime_connect` to the new path (321).**\n   Drop the connections-array copy-and-publish. Drop the\n   `_Atomic` qualifiers.\n9. **Add `runtime_destroy_box` and the language bridges (322).**\n10. **Add the strict-delete knob.**\n11. **Write fixtures** for each path (the seven in 320, the\n    two in 322).\n12. **Update `docs/datapaths.md` (or equivalent)** with the\n    new ASCII diagrams of dataflow.\n\n---\n\n## Part H — summary table of atomic ops\n\n| field | type | writer | reader | ordering |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| graph.boxes[id] | `_Atomic(box_t *)` | mutator (swap) | worker (lookup) | release / acquire |\n| box.gather_flag | `_Atomic int` | worker (CAS 0↔1) | worker (same CAS) | acquire on win / release on free |\n| box.refcount | `_Atomic int` | worker + mutator (fetch_add/sub) | freer (post-sub) | acquire on bump / release on drop |\n| slot ordering-ring head | per-slot lock | producer (push) | consumer (peek/pop) | per-slot spinlock |\n| ring data cell (RING_RECONFIGURE) | per-slot lock | producer (push of `box_t *` bytes) | consumer (pop, holds gather flag) | per-slot spinlock + gather flag |\n| box.n_connections / .connections | plain (after 321) | mutator (writes NEW pre-swap) | consumer (reads via NEW after swap) | happens-before via slot release/acquire |\n\nAll of these except the per-slot spinlocks are lock-free.\nThe per-slot spinlocks already exist (issue 302 territory)\nand aren't changed.\n\n---\n\n## Part I — what changes in which file\n\n- `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}` — add RING_RECONFIGURE (and\n  maybe RING_DELETE) to the tag enum; the dual-ring push /\n  pop / peek helpers grow new variants for the control\n  tag.\n- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — make the `boxes` array's\n  cell type `_Atomic(box_t *)`; add `gather_flag` and\n  `refcount` fields to `box_t`; drop the `_Atomic`\n  qualifiers on `n_connections` and `connections` (after\n  321); add a `box_t *` free helper that's the right\n  shape for the refcount-driven path.\n- `src/012-dispatch.{c,h}` — wrap the action with the\n  gather CAS and refcount bump; add the mutator branch;\n  add the BOX_UTILITY case (replacing or subsuming\n  BOX_CREATE_BOX and BOX_CONNECT).\n- `src/013-jsonl-events.{c,h}` /\n  `src/014-event-queue.{c,h}` — add the three new event\n  types (`box_reconfigure`, `reconfigure_discard`,\n  `box_delete`) and the new `target-gone` push result.\n- `src/018-runtime-builtins.{c,h}` — re-implement\n  `runtime_connect` over the new path; add\n  `runtime_apply_box_spec` and `runtime_destroy_box` as\n  internal entry points; the C glue for the language\n  bridges.\n- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c`\n  — one new bridge function each: `destroy_box`.\n\n---\n\n## End-state, in one sentence\n\nA box's identity is owned by a per-id atomic pointer in\nthe graph slot; mutation happens by building a fully-\ntyped replacement off the hot path, pushing its pointer\nthrough an input wire tagged as control, and letting the\ntarget's next worker do the swap under a per-box gather\nlock with a refcount to govern the old record's free. The\nsame mechanism handles create / reconfigure / delete, and\nthe same mechanism absorbs the connections-append\noperation that used to be its own special case.\n",
    "userModified":false
  },
```

```json
        "text":"Done. Plan is at `/tmp/soramech-320-321-322-plan.md`.\n\nThe doc covers, in order: the shape in cleartext (Part A), the six atomic-state pieces and their orderings (Part B), the byte/struct/tag shapes on the wires (Part C), the worker-arrival flow with each atomic op marked (Part D), user-facing scenarios for each operation (Part E), eight open questions (Part F), a draft implementation order (Part G), and a one-table summary of every atomic op (Part H).\n\n`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`\n- The whole design rests on a small trio of atomics — the per-id graph-slot pointer, the per-box gather flag, and the per-box refcount — coordinating through release/acquire pairs. The gather flag doesn't serialise pushes (those stay per-slot); it only serialises the consumer's peek-then-pop window. That's what makes the producer side lock-free.\n- The RING_RECONFIGURE tag is doing the same job as RING_NATIVE / RING_JSON: a discriminator on the ordering ring that says \"interpret this cell as type X.\" The third tag is a tiny addition that lets control messages ride the existing transport instead of needing a separate queue — the design's main simplification over the earlier per-box control-queue sketch.\n- Mutator's \"+1 hold\" on refcount is the subtle bit: without it, the freer could observe refcount==0 between the swap and the mutator's own decrement. The hold means the mutator is itself a refcount holder, so the box record only frees AFTER the mutator's work happens-before whatever frees it.\n`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`\n\nThe biggest open questions I'd like your call on before implementation:\n\n- **F1**: single-channel (the wire IS the control message, mixed semantics) vs separate-ports (one wire per op type). Single is what 320 currently describes; separate is cleaner per-wire.\n- **F4**: name for the new box kind — `utility-box`, `spec-applier`, `box-mutate`, `box-control`?\n- **F6**: keep `soramech.connect` synchronous via a same-task fast-path, or accept the new async semantics?\n- **F8**: do we accept the slot leak on abandoned ports, or is slot_free a prerequisite?\n\nWhich of those do you want to talk through first?"
```
